## Meaning of SULSUL (술술)

The word ‘SULSUL’ (술술) in Korean means flowing smoothly, without getting stuck.

I hope all learners reading this book and studying with the SULSUL Korean app will eventually speak Korean smoothly and fluently (SULSUL). 

---

## Dedication

This book is dedicated to everyone who truly meant it whenever they said, “I want to learn Korean.”

That short phrase already held endless possibilities.

This book is my answer to those possibilities.

---

## How to Use This Book

### What is this book?

This is not your traditional Korean grammar textbook.

**‘SULSUL Korean’** is a**pattern-based acquisition system designed to help you learn real-life, practical Korean in the fastest way possible**, whether you are a complete beginner or an intermediate learner hitting a wall in speaking. The goal is not to memorize grammar for a test, but to build your speaking skills so they work naturally in real conversations.

### Who is this book for?

- Complete beginners starting Korean for the very first time.
- Fans of K-dramas and K-pop who suddenly want to learn the language.
- Those who have studied with traditional textbooks but feel frustrated because they can’t speak.
- Students currently taking lessons with a native Korean tutor.

## 📖 PART 1. Who Are You, Korean?

— What You Must Know Before Learning Korean

Many people say this when they first start learning Korean: “There’s way too much grammar.” “I want to speak, but my mouth just won’t open.”

This isn’t because Korean is a particularly ‘more complicated’ language. In most cases, it’s because people start studying it using the exact same methods they used for other languages, without considering the unique structure and nature of Korean.

Korean is a language that unlocks the slowest when you approach it with the same structure as English or European languages. Conversely, it is also the language where you can speed up the fastest when you approach it with a method tailored to its true nature.

### 1. Korean is a language of ‘Relationships and Emotions’, not just ‘Information’

Many languages operate by transferring information. The most important information in a sentence is “what,” and the sentence exists to convey that information accurately.

However, Korean is a little different. The core of Korean isn’t “what,” but rather **“who is speaking to whom, and with what emotion and warmth.”** In other words, Korean has a structure where relationships and feelings take priority over mere facts.

### 2. Language learning is ‘Acquisition’, not memorization

There is a common principle shared by polyglots all over the world: “Language is not something you study; it is something you acquire.” A language is not information you memorize and store for a test.

It’s a process where patterns naturally form in your brain as you listen, see, and speak a lot. This process consists of three steps: Input → Pattern Recognition → Output (Speaking).

## 📖 PART 2. The Fastest Way to Learn Korean

— A 3-Step Approach to Hacking the Language Code

Acquiring a language is like learning an instrument or playing a sport. It requires a process that goes beyond understanding it in your head; you have to make your body and mouth remember it.

### Step 1: Overwhelming Input

You need to frequently expose your brain to a natural Korean environment. Use your favorite content like dramas, variety shows, or music. You don’t need to know every single word. The key is to just ‘feel’ the rhythm, intonation, and nuance of the language fitting the situation.

### Step 2: Core Pattern Recognition

This is the stage where you discover the recurring frameworks amidst the overwhelming input. The 100 essential patterns in this book are exactly those frameworks! Instead of blindly memorizing words, practice learning the most frequently used “pattern blocks” and swapping different words into them. (e.g., 사과 (sa-gwa), 가방 (ga-bang))

### Step 3: Your Own Output

This is the time to turn the patterns you’ve learned into stories from your own life and spit them out. Try creating sentences while wondering, “How can I use this pattern on my friend tomorrow?” It’s okay to make mistakes. The very moment you speak it out loud, it truly becomes your language.

## 📖 PART 3. Building Your Own Accelerated Learning System

— 1% Class Time and 99% Self-Study Time

Anyone learning a language asks this question at least once: “Will I improve faster if I increase my class time?” It might seem obvious on the outside, but reality is a bit different. Language doesn’t just grow while sitting at a desk. Real skills are usually forged outside of class hours.

### Why is class time only 1%?

Class is not the time to “complete” your language. The space where language is completed is always within the student’s daily life. A tutor is someone who shines a light to show you the path. Walking down that path holding the light is entirely up to you, the learner.

Listening for 10~15 minutes a day, practicing speaking one or two patterns out loud using the**SULSUL App**, and creating 2~3 sentences that fit your life. When this simple routine accumulates, it triggers a powerful transformation. Take what you learned with your 선생님 (seon-saeng-nim) and bring it into your everyday life!

## 📖 PART 4. Hangul (The Code): Blocks of Sound

**— The First Chapter to Open the Door to Korean with 40 Sounds**

This chapter is a ‘Hangul Guide’ for those of you starting Korean for the first time. Hangul is the most scientific and easiest-to-learn writing system in the world. Don’t stress over tongue positions or complicated pronunciation symbols!

Please do not analyze the letters with your eyes, but just focus on hearing the sounds in chunks and repeating them.

### 1. Hangul is like ‘Lego Blocks’

The English alphabet lines up letters in a long row to the side, right? (e.g.: s-t-u-d-e-n-t)

On the other hand, Hangul gathers sounds top, bottom, and side to side, assembling them into a single ‘block (syllable)’.

Blocks are made like this:

- **Initial Consonant (First sound - Consonant)**
- **Medial Vowel (Middle sound - Vowel)**
- **Batchim (Ending sound - may or may not be present)**

Let’s look at an example:

- 가 = ㄱ (Initial Consonant) + ㅏ (Medial Vowel)
- 밤 = ㅂ (Initial Consonant) + ㅏ (Medial Vowel) + ㅁ (Batchim)

Hangul is not a writing system where you read each alphabet letter individually, but one where you sound out these assembled “sound bundles” all at once.

### 2. 19 Consonants — Sounds that Mimic Mouth Shapes

Consonants are sounds made when the lips or tongue touch during pronunciation.

There are 14 basic consonants and 5 double consonants pronounced strongly. Try writing them lightly while making the sounds.

**[14 Basic Consonants]**

- **ㄱ (g/k):** 가방 (ga-bang), 국 (guk)
- **ㄴ (n):** 나무 (na-mu)
- **ㄷ (d/t):** 다리 (da-ri)
- **ㄹ (r/l):** 라면 (ra-myeon)
- **ㅁ (m):** 머리 (meo-ri)
- **ㅂ (b/p):** 바나나 (ba-na-na)
- **ㅅ (s):** 사랑 (sa-rang)
- **ㅇ (silent/ng):** It has no sound at the very front of a letter, and only makes an ‘ng’ sound when it comes at the very bottom (Batchim). 아이 (a-i), 영어 (yeong-eo)
- **ㅈ (j):** 자동차 (ja-dong-cha)
- **ㅊ (ch):** 친구 (chin-gu)
- **ㅋ (k):** 커피 (keo-pi)
- **ㅌ (t):** 토마토 (to-ma-to)
- **ㅍ (p):** 포도 (po-do)
- **ㅎ (h):** 하늘 (ha-neul)

**[5 Double Consonants (Solid and Strong Sounds)]**

These are not dragged-out sounds! Put tension in your mouth and cut them off short and solid.

- **ㄲ (kk):** 꼬리 (kko-ri)
- **ㄸ (tt):** 땀 (ttam)
- **ㅃ (pp):** 빵 (ppang)
- **ㅆ (ss):** 쌀 (ssal)
- **ㅉ (jj):** 짜다 (jja-da)

### 3. 21 Vowels — Sky, Earth, Human

Depending on their shape, Hangul vowels are written either to the**side** or**below** the consonant.

**[Vertical Vowels: Sounds standing to the ‘side’ of the consonant]**

- **ㅏ (a):** 사과 (sa-gwa)
- **ㅑ (ya):** 야구 (ya-gu)
- **ㅓ (eo):** 머리 (meo-ri)
- **ㅕ (yeo):** 여행 (yeo-haeng)
- **ㅣ (i):** 이름 (i-reum)
- **ㅐ/ㅔ (ae/e):** 개 (gae), 세수 (se-su) *The pronunciation is almost exactly the same in modern Korean!*

**[Horizontal Vowels: Sounds laid ‘below’ the consonant]**

- **ㅗ (o):** 고기 (go-gi)
- **ㅛ (yo):** 요리 (yo-ri)
- **ㅜ (u):** 우유 (u-yu)
- **ㅠ (yu):** 휴가 (hyu-ga)
- **ㅡ (eu):** 크다 (keu-da)

**[Complex Vowels: Sounds where horizontal and vertical meet]**

These are sounds like ‘와 (wa)’ or ‘의 (ui)’. Lay the horizontal vowel below first, and stand the vertical vowel to the side.

- **ㅘ (wa):** 과자 (gwa-ja)
- **ㅚ / ㅙ / ㅞ (we):** 외국 (woe-guk), 왜 (wae) *They are all pronounced similar to ‘웨 (we)’!*
- **ㅟ (wi):** 귀 (gwi)
- **ㅢ (ui):** 의사 (ui-sa)

### 4. Batchim — The Door that Closes the Sound

The consonant that goes at the very bottom of a letter block is called ‘Batchim’. When a Batchim is added, your mouth closes, and it feels like the sound shuts with a snap!

There are many Batchims you can write as letters, but the sounds we actually hear with our ears are**only 7 types**. Don’t memorize them in a complicated way!

1.**[k] sound (ㄱ, ㅋ, ㄲ):** 국 (guk), 부엌 (bu-eok)
2.**[n] sound (ㄴ):** 눈 (nun)
3.**[t] sound (ㄷ, ㅅ, ㅈ, ㅊ, ㅌ, ㅎ):** 옷 (ot), 낮 (nat), 꽃 (kkot) *They all look different, but the pronunciation is all ‘t’!*
4. **[l] sound (ㄹ):** 길 (gil)
5.**[m] sound (ㅁ):** 밤 (bam)
6.**[p] sound (ㅂ, ㅍ):** 밥 (bap), 잎 (ip)
7.**[ng] sound (ㅇ):** 방 (bang)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Reading Aloud)**
> 
> Do not just look with your eyes, but make sure to voice the sounds. Try practicing reading “가, 나, 다, 라…” smoothly all at once while riding the rhythm for just 5 minutes a day. The letters will gradually stick to your mouth!
>
>  

## 📖 PART 5. The Melody of Korean (Sound Tuning)

**— Escaping the Robot Sound and Riding the Korean Rhythm**

Just because you know how to read Hangul doesn’t mean you can speak Korean. If you read “안녕하세요” clearly and stiffly just as it’s written in the book, Korean people will feel like you’re a robot. Why? Because there’s no ‘rhythm’!

In this chapter, we will tune your pronunciation to ‘human mode’.

### 1. Riding the Sound Slide (Liaison)

Korean is a block language, but when making sounds, you have to break down the walls between the blocks. If the front letter has a Batchim and the back letter starts with ‘ㅇ (silent vowel)’, the Batchim moves over to the back.

- **밥을 먹어요**
    
    Letters: 밥-을 먹-어-요
    
    Sound:**[바블 머거요]**
    
- **음악**
    
    Letters: 음-악
    
    Sound:**[으막]**
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] Do not read them choppily! The key is to connect the sounds smoothly like riding a slide.
> 

### 2. Strong for Important Words, Weak for Particles

Korean has ‘particles (은/는, 이/가)’ that help with grammar, right? Particles are like the ‘shadows’ of words. So if you pronounce the particle too loudly, it sounds very awkward.

- 
   - Robot pronunciation: 저-는 학-생-이-에-요 (giving equal force to every letter)
- 
    - Natural pronunciation:**저**는**학생**이에요 (relax the particle and slip it onto the preceding word)

### 3. Raise the End for a Question, Drag it for Emotion!

In Korean, the nuance completely changes depending on how you handle the end of a sentence.

- **Lowering (↘):** Declarative sentence. Drop it confidently. (e.g.: 밥 먹었어요 ↘)
- **Raising (↗):** Question. Raise the end clearly. (e.g.: 밥 먹었어요? ↗)
- **Dragging long (→):** Expressing emotion. Stretching the middle or end carries emotion. (e.g.: 진~짜요? / 아~ 그렇구나)

Now, you are ready to play the melody of Korean. Shall we learn the patterns for survival in earnest?

## 📖 PART 6. Survival Engine

**— ‘Chunks’ to Survive Immediately Without Grammar**

The first thing a soldier going into the battlefield needs is not to learn the structure of a gun. It’s how to pull the trigger immediately! Learning Korean is the same. There’s no time to figure out the order of subject and verb. Because if you’re hungry, you have to ask for food, and if it’s urgent, you have to find a restroom.

In this chapter, we will equip 4 survival engines that will solve 80% of your life in Korea. Don’t calculate with your head, but spit them out as chunks until your mouth remembers!

---

![[sulsul_pattern_01.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 명사 Noun ]** +**주세요.**
- *“Please give me [Noun].”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is a very useful survival cheat code you can use anywhere, like restaurants, cafes, or convenience stores, to request what you want. Even if you don’t know the word, just pointing with your finger and saying “이거 주세요” will get your point across!

### 3) Grammar

- It’s very simple! Just attach ‘주세요’ right after a noun.
- Originally, you should use a particle like ‘물을 주세요’, but in actual conversation, it’s much more natural to speak without the particle.
- **The magic word ‘좀 (jom)’:** Try inserting ‘좀’ between the noun and ‘주세요’. Saying “물 좀 주세요” acts as a soft cushion that prevents it from sounding like an order.

### 4) How it’s used

- At a restaurant: When ordering from the menu, when asking for water
- When shopping: When pointing at an item to express your intention to buy
- In a taxi: When stating your destination, saying “강남역으로 가주세요”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**물** (Water),**메뉴판** (Menu board),**이거** (This),**저거** (That),**영수증** (Receipt),**봉투** (Plastic bag),**휴지** (Tissue),**밥** (Rice)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **물 주세요.** (Water, please.)
- **메뉴판 주세요.** (Menu, please.)
- **이거 주세요.** (This one, please.)
- **저거 주세요.** (That one, please.)
- **커피 주세요.** (Coffee, please.)
- **휴지 주세요.** (Tissue, please.)
- **영수증 주세요.** (Receipt, please.)
- **봉투 주세요.** (Bag, please.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **물 좀 주세요.** (Please give me some water.)
- **따뜻한 물 주세요.** (Warm water, please.)
- **아메리카노 한 잔 주세요.** (One Americano, please.)
- **이거 두 개 주세요.** (Give me two of these, please.)
- **김치 좀 더 주세요.** (Please give me some more Kimchi.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Fill in the blanks (Say it with the word that fits the situation)

1. (When you’re thirsty at a restaurant) _______________ 주세요.
2. (When shopping, pointing at clothes) _______________ 주세요.
3. (After finishing payment) _______________ 주세요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 
> 
> 1. 물 좀 / 2. 이거 / 3. 영수증
> 

### 9) My Sentence

What do you need right now? Try making your own sentence!

- _______________ 주세요.
- _______________ 좀 주세요.

---

![[sulsul_pattern_02.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**고 싶어요.**
- *“I want to [Verb].”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is a method of conveying your inner wishes or desires to the other person. Use this pattern when you want to eat something or go somewhere.

### 3) Grammar

- Just take off ‘다’ from the original form (dictionary form) of the verb (Verb Stem), and attach ‘고 싶어요’ in its place.
- 가다 (to go) → 가 + 고 싶어요 → **가고 싶어요.**
- 먹다 (to eat) → 먹 + 고 싶어요 →**먹고 싶어요.**
- It’s a really easy pattern because the form doesn’t change whether there is a Batchim or not!

### 4) How it’s used

- When hungry: “비빔밥 먹고 싶어요.”
- When tired: “집에 가고 싶어요.”
- When you miss someone: “보고 싶어요.” (It carries both meanings of ‘See’ and ‘Miss’!)

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**가다** (Go),**먹다** (Eat),**자다** (Sleep),**쉬다** (Rest),**사다** (Buy),**보다** (See/Watch/Miss),**하다** (Do)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **가고 싶어요.** (I want to go.)
- **먹고 싶어요.** (I want to eat.)
- **자고 싶어요.** (I want to sleep.)
- **쉬고 싶어요.** (I want to rest.)
- **보고 싶어요.** (I want to see/watch OR I miss you.)
- **하고 싶어요.** (I want to do it.)
- **사고 싶어요.** (I want to buy it.)
- **놀고 싶어요.** (I want to play/hang out.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **집에 가고 싶어요.** (I want to go home.)
- **삼겹살 먹고 싶어요.** (I want to eat Samgyeopsal.)
- **한국어 공부하고 싶어요.** (I want to study Korean.)
- **영화 보고 싶어요.** (I want to watch a movie.)
- **주말에 푹 쉬고 싶어요.** (I want to get a good rest this weekend.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Change the basic verbs to fit the form.

1. 마시다 → _______________
2. 만나다 → _______________
3. 여행하다 → _______________
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 
> 
> 마시고 싶어요 / 2. 만나고 싶어요 / 3. 여행하고 싶어요
> 

### 9) My Sentence

What is the thing you most want to do this evening?

- 저는 오늘 _______________고 싶어요.

---

![[sulsul_pattern_03.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 명사 Noun ]** +**있어요? / 없어요?**
- *“Do you have [Noun]? / Is there [Noun]?”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is a perfect pattern to use when asking if a certain item exists in this space, or if the other person has something. When there is something you are looking for, this one question solves everything!

### 3) Grammar

- Attach ‘있어요?’ or ‘없어요?’ right after the item or target you are looking for.
- For this pattern too, it is much smoother to speak without the particle in everyday conversation. (시간이 있어요? → 시간 있어요?)
- If you raise the end to say **“있어요? ↗”** it becomes a question, and if you lower the end to say**“있어요. ↘”** it becomes an answer.

### 4) How it’s used

- When looking for items in a store: “이거 다른 색깔 있어요?”
- When making plans: “오늘 저녁에 시간 있어요?”
- When talking about a lack of something: “현금 없어요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**시간** (Time),**돈** (Money),**화장실** (Restroom),**와이파이** (Wi-Fi),**자리** (Seat),**남자친구/여자친구** (BF/GF),**질문** (Question)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **시간 있어요?** (Do you have time?)
- **돈 있어요?** (Do you have money?)
- **화장실 있어요?** (Is there a restroom?)
- **자리 있어요?** (Are there any seats?)
- **와이파이 있어요?** (Is there Wi-Fi?)
- **시간 없어요.** (I don’t have time.)
- **현금 없어요.** (I don’t have cash.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **오늘 저녁에 약속 있어요?** (Do you have plans tonight?)
- **혹시 우산 있어요?** (By any chance, do you have an umbrella?)
- **매운 소스 있어요?** (Do you have spicy sauce?)
- **미안해요, 내일은 시간 없어요.** (Sorry, I don’t have time tomorrow.)
- **이거 더 큰 사이즈 없어요?** (Do you not have a bigger size of this?)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Ask questions by inserting words that fit the situation.

1. (When you entered a restaurant) _______________ 있어요? (자리)
2. (When you want to use the internet) _______________ 있어요? (와이파이)
3. (When asking someone on a date) 주말에 _______________ 있어요? (시간)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 
> 
> 자리 / 2. 와이파이 / 3. 시간
> 

### 9) My Sentence

What is something you are looking for right now, or something you need?

- 저기요, 혹시 _______________ 있어요?

---

![[sulsul_pattern_04.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 장소 명사 Place Noun ]** +**어디 있어요? / 어디예요?**
- *“Where is [Place]?”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is a savior-like expression necessary when you’re lost or asking for the location of a place or item. Don’t ask in a complicated way like “~~로 가는 길 좀 알려주시겠어요?”. Tossing just the most important word and asking “어디예요?” is the best!

### 3) Grammar

- Attach ‘어디 있어요?’ or even shorter ‘어디예요?’ after the place noun.
- ‘어디 있어요?’ can be used for both items (내 가방 어디 있어요?) and places, and ‘어디예요?’ is mainly used more casually when looking for a place.

### 4) How it’s used

- When urgently looking for a restroom
- When looking for a subway station or bus stop
- When looking for an entrance or exit inside a building

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**화장실** (Restroom),**입구** (Entrance),**출구** (Exit),**지하철역** (Subway station),**편의점** (Convenience store),**약국** (Pharmacy),**여기** (Here)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **화장실 어디 있어요?** (Where is the restroom?)
- **출구 어디예요?** (Where is the exit?)
- **지하철역 어디 있어요?** (Where is the subway station?)
- **편의점 어디예요?** (Where is the convenience store?)
- **약국 어디 있어요?** (Where is the pharmacy?)
- **여기 어디예요?** (Where is here? / Where am I?)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **저기요, 화장실 어디 있어요?** (Excuse me, where is the restroom?)
- **가까운 은행 어디예요?** (Where is the nearest bank?)
- **택시 타는 곳 어디 있어요?** (Where is the taxi stand?)
- **내 핸드폰 어디 있어요?** (Where is my phone?)
- **계산대 어디예요?** (Where is the checkout counter?)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Fill in the blanks to ask for directions.

1. (When you need to buy medicine) _______________ 어디예요?
2. (When you need to board a plane) 공항 _______________?
3. (When you need to throw away trash) 쓰레기통 _______________?
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 
> 
> 약국 / 2. 어디예요 / 3. 어디 있어요
> 

### 9) My Sentence

Imagine you are lost while traveling in Korea. What would you want to ask people?

- 죄송한데요, _______________ 어디예요?

![[sulsul_pattern_05.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 명사 Noun ]** +**이에요 / 예요.**
- *“It is [Noun] / I am [Noun].”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is a very basic and important expression used to introduce yourself or define what an object right in front of you is. You can think of it easily as playing the same role as the ‘be verb’ (am, is, are) in English.

### 3) Grammar

- The form changes depending on whether the last letter of the noun has a Batchim (bottom consonant) or not.
- **When there is a Batchim:** [Noun] + 이에요 (e.g., 학생 + 이에요 =**학생이에요**)
- **When there is NO Batchim:** [Noun] + 예요 (e.g., 사과 + 예요 =**사과예요**)
- The exact same rule applies when saying your name. You can use it like “저는 마이클이에요” (I am Michael) or “저는 제니예요” (I am Jenny).

### 4) How it’s used

- When introducing yourself: Saying your name, nationality, or job.
- When explaining an object: “이거 제 가방이에요.”
- When talking about relationships: “제 친구예요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**학생** (Student),**선생님** (Teacher),**회사원** (Office worker),**의사** (Doctor),**친구** (Friend),**미국 사람** (American),**한국 사람** (Korean),**저** (Me/I)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **학생이에요.** (I am a student.)
- **선생님이에요.** (I am a teacher.)
- **회사원이에요.** (I am an office worker.)
- **의사예요.** (I am a doctor.)
- **저예요.** (It’s me.)
- **한국 사람이에요.** (I am Korean.)
- **미국 사람이에요.** (I am American.)
- **제 친구예요.** (It’s my friend.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **이거 제 가방이에요.** (This is my bag.)
- **여기는 학교예요.** (This is a school.)
- **저 마이클이에요.** (I am Michael.)
- **오늘 제 생일이에요.** (Today is my birthday.)
- **이거 뭐예요?** (What is this? - Raise the end of the sentence when asking a question ↗)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Find the correct match. (Check for the Batchim!)

1. 저는 김민수 ( 이에요 / 예요 ).
2. 저는 마리아 ( 이에요 / 예요 ).
3. 이것은 핸드폰 ( 이에요 / 예요 ).
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 예요 / 2. 예요 / 3. 이에요

### 9) My Sentence

Who are you? Define yourself.

- 저는 _______________이에요 / 예요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_06.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 명사 Noun ]** +**해요.**
- *“I do [Noun] / I am doing [Noun].”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is the most energetic pattern used to say that you “do” a certain action or movement. In Korean, there are so many words that turn into verbs just by adding ‘하다’ after a noun. Once you know this pattern, you’ll be able to easily express countless actions.

### 3) Grammar

- The original verb form ‘하다 (to do)’ changes to ‘해요’ in conversation. You just attach it right after nouns that represent actions like studying, working, or exercising.
- You can insert the object particle ‘을/를’ in the middle like ‘공부를 해요’, but in everyday conversation, it sounds much more natural to drop the particle and attach it directly like **‘공부해요’**!

### 4) How it’s used

- When talking about your daily routine.
- When talking about hobbies or favorite activities.
- When talking about an action you are doing right now.

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**공부** (Study),**일** (Work),**운동** (Exercise),**요리** (Cooking),**청소** (Cleaning),**운전** (Driving),**전화** (Phone call),**샤워** (Shower),**쇼핑** (Shopping)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **공부해요.** (I study.)
- **일해요.** (I work.)
- **운동해요.** (I exercise.)
- **요리해요.** (I cook.)
- **청소해요.** (I clean.)
- **운전해요.** (I drive.)
- **전화해요.** (I call.)
- **샤워해요.** (I take a shower.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **지금 공부해요.** (I am studying now.)
- **내일 일해요.** (I will work tomorrow.)
- **매일 운동해요.** (I exercise every day.)
- **주말에 쇼핑해요.** (I go shopping on weekends.)
- **친구랑 전화해요.** (I talk on the phone with a friend.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Change the noun into a verb (action).

1. 수영 (Swimming) → _______________
2. 노래 (Singing) → _______________
3. 숙제 (Homework) → _______________
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 수영해요 / 2. 노래해요 / 3. 숙제해요

### 9) My Sentence

What do you usually do every day?

- 저는 매일 _______________해요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_07.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **안** +**[ 형용사/동사 Adjective/Verb ]**
- **못** +**[ 동사 Verb ]**
- *“Do not / Cannot [Verb/Adjective]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is the pattern used when expressing situations where you don’t do something, or you cannot do something.

- **안 (An):** Used when you reject or deny something by your own will, meaning “I won’t do it!” (I don’t)
- **못 (Mot):** Used to make an excuse like “I can’t do it…” due to circumstances or lack of ability, regardless of your will. (I can’t)

### 3) Grammar

- Just put ‘안’ or ‘못’ briefly in front of the word, and you’re done!
- 가요 →**안 가요** (I won’t go) /**못 가요** (I want to go, but I can’t because I have something to do)
- **Caution:** When negating verbs in the form of ‘Noun + 하다(해요)’, it is natural to put it between the noun and ‘하다’! (e.g., 일 안 해요 (O) / 안 일해요 (X))
- Since ‘못’ talks about ability, you CANNOT use it in front of adjectives (like 예쁘다, 바쁘다)!

### 4) How it’s used

- When rejecting a suggestion: “고기 안 먹어요.”
- When explaining a lack of ability: “수영 못 해요.”
- When negating a state: “안 매워요.” (It’s not spicy.)

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**가다** (Go),**먹다** (Eat),**하다** (Do),**맵다** (Spicy),**비싸다** (Expensive),**바쁘다** (Busy)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **안 가요.** (I’m not going.)
- **못 가요.** (I can’t go.)
- **안 먹어요.** (I don’t eat it.)
- **못 먹어요.** (I can’t eat it.)
- **안 해요.** (I won’t do it.)
- **못 해요.** (I can’t do it / I’m bad at it.)
- **안 매워요.** (It’s not spicy.)
- **안 비싸요.** (It’s not expensive.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **저는 아침을 안 먹어요.** (I don’t eat breakfast.)
- **오늘 바빠서 못 만나요.** (I am busy today, so I can’t meet you.)
- **한국말 잘 못 해요.** (I can’t speak Korean well.)
- **매운 음식 못 먹어요.** (I can’t eat spicy food - due to allergies or other reasons)
- **오늘 일 안 해요.** (I don’t work today.)

> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher's Tip**
>
> In real Korean conversations, native speakers often **drop the subject '저는 (I)'** when talking about themselves — it's naturally implied from context! So instead of "저는 아침을 안 먹어요", you'll just as commonly hear **"아침 안 먹어요"**. Both are perfectly correct, but the shorter version has that effortless, natural-speaker energy. As your Korean gets more comfortable, try dropping that '저는' and notice how much more fluently it flows!

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Choose the correct negative word.

1. 저는 해산물 알레르기가 있어서 새우를 ( 안 / 못 ) 먹어요.
2. 다이어트 중이라서 저녁을 ( 안 / 못 ) 먹어요.
3. 이 옷은 하나도 ( 안 / 못 ) 비싸요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 못 / 2. 안 / 3. 안

### 9) My Sentence

What is something you ‘안’ (don’t) do because you don’t like it, and what is something you ‘못’ (can’t) do because it’s difficult?

- 저는 _______________을/를 안 해요.
- 저는 _______________을/를 못 해요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_08.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**았/었어요.**
- *“Did [Verb] / [Verb]-ed.”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is the pattern used when talking about past events that happened yesterday, last week, or a little while ago. The core of the Korean past tense is the double consonant **‘ㅆ’ (ss)** sound heard in the middle of the sentence! If you hear this sound, it’s 100% a story from the past.

### 3) Grammar

- The form is determined by what the last vowel of the Verb Stem is.
- **When the last vowel is ‘ㅏ, ㅗ’:** + 았어요 (e.g., 가다 → 가 + 았어요 =**갔어요**, 보다 → 보 + 았어요 =**봤어요**)
- **For all other vowels:** + 었어요 (e.g., 먹다 → 먹 + 었어요 =**먹었어요**)
- **‘하다’ verbs:** They unconditionally change to**‘했어요’**! (e.g., 공부하다 → 공부했어요)

### 4) How it’s used

- When talking about what you did yesterday or on the weekend.
- When asking if something has been completed, like “밥 먹었어요?”.
- When sharing past experiences.

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**어제** (Yesterday),**아까** (A while ago),**지난주** (Last week),**주말에** (On the weekend),**아침에** (In the morning)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **갔어요.** (I went.)
- **왔어요.** (I came.)
- **먹었어요.** (I ate.)
- **봤어요.** (I saw / I watched.)
- **만났어요.** (I met.)
- **공부했어요.** (I studied.)
- **일했어요.** (I worked.)
- **운동했어요.** (I exercised.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **어제 피자 먹었어요.** (I ate pizza yesterday.)
- **주말에 친구 만났어요.** (I met a friend on the weekend.)
- **한국 드라마 봤어요.** (I watched a Korean drama.)
- **아까 운동했어요.** (I exercised earlier.)
- **아침에 커피 마셨어요.** (I drank coffee in the morning.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Change the present tense to the past tense.

1. 가요 → 갔어요
2. 먹어요 → _______________
3. 해요 → _______________
4. 만나요 → _______________
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 2. 먹었어요 / 3. 했어요 / 4. 만났어요

### 9) My Sentence

What did you do yesterday?

- 저는 어제 _______________았/었어요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_09.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**ㄹ/을 거예요.**
- *“Will [Verb] / Am going to [Verb].”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is the pattern used when talking about things you will do tomorrow, future plans, or making a firm resolution saying, “I will definitely do this!” The core of the Korean future tense is the **‘ㄹ’** Batchim!

### 3) Grammar

- Choose based on whether the Verb Stem has a Batchim or not.
- **When there is NO Batchim:** + ㄹ 거예요 (e.g., 가다 → 가 + ㄹ 거예요 =**갈 거예요**)
- **When there is a Batchim:** + 을 거예요 (e.g., 먹다 → 먹 + 을 거예요 =**먹을 거예요**)
- **Exception (‘ㄹ’ Batchim):** Since it already has a ‘ㄹ’, just attach ‘거예요’ directly. (e.g., 놀다 →**놀 거예요**)

### 4) How it’s used

- When talking about weekend plans or vacation schedules.
- When stating your resolution (“I will definitely do it!”).
- When predicting something that will happen in the future.

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**내일** (Tomorrow),**주말에** (On the weekend),**나중에** (Later),**이따가** (A bit later today),**곧** (Soon)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **갈 거예요.** (I will go.)
- **할 거예요.** (I will do.)
- **먹을 거예요.** (I will eat.)
- **볼 거예요.** (I will watch/see.)
- **만날 거예요.** (I will meet.)
- **살 거예요.** (I will buy.)
- **쉴 거예요.** (I will rest.)
- **잘 거예요.** (I will sleep.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **내일 한국에 갈 거예요.** (I will go to Korea tomorrow.)
- **저녁에 불고기 먹을 거예요.** (I will eat Bulgogi for dinner.)
- **주말에 푹 쉴 거예요.** (I am going to get a good rest this weekend.)
- **이따가 전화할 거예요.** (I will call you later.)
- **나중에 다시 올 거예요.** (I will come back later.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Change to future plans.

1. 사다 (Buy) → 살 거예요.
2. 읽다 (Read) → _______________
3. 쉬다 (Rest) → _______________
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 2. 읽을 거예요 / 3. 쉴 거예요

### 9) My Sentence

What do you plan to do this coming weekend?

- 저는 주말에 _______________ㄹ/을 거예요.

---

## 💎 Special Unit 1. 나의 몸과 상태 (My Body & Health)

"머리가 아파요", "눈이 예뻐요"

The most useful moment after learning adjectives is when you talk about 'your body'. Let's learn essential words needed when you suddenly get sick while traveling and have to go to the pharmacy or hospital, or when you want to compliment a friend's appearance.

**1. 내 몸의 지도 (Body Map)**

There is a Korean children's song that goes "머리~ 어깨 무릎 발~" (Head~ shoulders knees toes~). If you just know these words, you can accurately tell the doctor where it hurts at the hospital.

- **얼굴 (Face):** 머리(Head/Hair), 눈(Eye), 코(Nose), 입(Mouth), 귀(Ear)

- **몸 (Body):** 손(Hand), 발(Foot), 배(Stomach/Belly), 다리(Leg), 허리(Waist/Back)

**2. 자주 쓰는 표현 (Common Expressions)**

- **아파요 (It hurts / I have pain):** 머리가 아파요. (I have a headache.) / 배가 아파요. (I have a stomachache.)

- **부었어요 (It is swollen):** 얼굴이 부었어요. (My face is swollen.) / 다리가 부었어요. (My legs are swollen.)

- **예뻐요 (It is pretty):** 눈이 예뻐요. (Your eyes are pretty.) / 코가 예뻐요. (Your nose is pretty.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher's Tip**
>
> In Korean, when you say "My head hurts", you don't necessarily have to add '나의 (My)' as in "나의 머리가". If you just say "머리가 아파요", it's naturally understood that your head hurts.

**3. Practice — 연습 문제**

✔**연습 1.** Playing Hospital (Tell the doctor your symptoms)

1. (Rubbing your stomach) 선생님, 저 _______________ 아파요.

2. (When you injured your leg) 자고 일어났더니 _______________ 부었어요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
>
> 1. 배가 / 2. 다리가

---


---


---


 

![[sulsul_pattern_10.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**고 있어요.**
- *“Am [Verb]-ing.”*

### 2) Core Meaning

It is used to express a vivid action happening right in front of your eyes at this very moment, neither past nor future. It’s the perfect pattern to answer when someone asks “지금 뭐 해요?” (What are you doing now?).

### 3) Grammar

- It’s really easy! Just unconditionally attach ‘고 있어요’ to the Verb Stem, whether it has a Batchim or not, and whether it’s irregular or not.
- 가다 → **가고 있어요.**
- 먹다 →**먹고 있어요.**
- 하다 →**하고 있어요.**

### 4) How it’s used

- When talking on the phone (“What are you doing right now?”).
- When you are on your way to an appointment (“I’m on my way now”).
- When talking about something you are doing repeatedly these days (“I’m learning Korean these days”).

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**지금** (Now),**기다리다** (Wait),**가다** (Go),**오다** (Come),**듣다** (Listen),**찾다** (Look for)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **가고 있어요.** (I am going / I’m on my way.)
- **오고 있어요.** (I am coming.)
- **먹고 있어요.** (I am eating.)
- **하고 있어요.** (I am doing it.)
- **보고 있어요.** (I am watching.)
- **자고 있어요.** (I am sleeping.)
- **기다리고 있어요.** (I am waiting.)
- **듣고 있어요.** (I am listening.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **지금 밥 먹고 있어요.** (I am eating a meal now.)
- **버스 기다리고 있어요.** (I am waiting for the bus.)
- **한국어 공부하고 있어요.** (I am studying Korean.)
- **집에 가고 있어요.** (I am going home.)
- **음악 듣고 있어요.** (I am listening to music.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** What are you doing now? Change the following verbs to the progressive form.

1. 운전하다 → _______________
2. 청소하다 → _______________
3. 만들다 → _______________
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 운전하고 있어요 / 2. 청소하고 있어요 / 3. 만들고 있어요

### 9) My Sentence

What are you doing right now? (You’re looking at this book, right?)

- 저는 지금 _______________고 있어요.

---

![[sulsul_pattern_11.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 형용사 어간 Adjective Stem ]** +**아/어요.**
- *“It is [Adjective] / I am [Adjective].”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Now we can talk about actions (Action). But humans are not robots; we feel emotions (Emotion)! This is the pattern used to describe your mood, the weather, the taste of food, etc. In Korean, adjectives act like verbs as the predicate, so you don’t need ‘be verbs’ (am/is/are).

### 3) Grammar

The rules are exactly the same as when making the past tense (았/었어요)!

- **When the last vowel is ‘ㅏ, ㅗ’:** + 아요 (e.g., 많다 →**많아요**, 좋다 →**좋아요**)
- **When the last vowel is NOT ‘ㅏ, ㅗ’:** + 어요 (e.g., 맛있다 →**맛있어요**, 적다 →**적어요**)
- **‘하다’ adjectives:** Change to**‘해요’**. (e.g., 피곤하다 →**피곤해요**)
- **ㅡ Drop:** For words ending in ‘ㅡ’ like ‘바쁘다’ or ‘아프다’, the ‘ㅡ’ disappears and ‘아/어’ is attached. (e.g., 아프다 →**아파요**, 바쁘다 →**바빠요**)

### 4) How it’s used

- When expressing the taste after eating food (“맛있어요!”).
- When talking about your current condition (“피곤해요”, “아파요”).
- When evaluating the design of an item or the weather (“예뻐요”, “추워요”).

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**좋다** (Good),**싫다** (Dislike),**맛있다** (Delicious),**재미있다** (Fun),**예쁘다** (Pretty),**비싸다** (Expensive),**바쁘다** (Busy),**피곤하다** (Tired),**아프다** (Sick/Hurt)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **좋아요.** (It’s good / I like it.)
- **싫어요.** (I dislike it.)
- **맛있어요.** (It’s delicious.)
- **재미있어요.** (It’s fun/interesting.)
- **예뻐요.** (It’s pretty.)
- **비싸요.** (It’s expensive.)
- **바빠요.** (I’m busy.)
- **피곤해요.** (I’m tired.)
- **아파요.** (It hurts / I’m sick.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **오늘 날씨가 참 좋아요.** (The weather is really good today.)
- **이 케이크 진짜 맛있어요.** (This cake is really delicious.)
- **저 지금 조금 바빠요.** (I am a little busy right now.)
- **오늘 많이 피곤해요.** (I am very tired today.)
- **머리가 너무 아파요.** (My head hurts a lot.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Find the antonym.

1. 좋아요 ↔︎ _______________
2. 바빠요 ↔︎ 한가해요 (Free)
3. 비싸요 ↔︎ _______________
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 싫어요 / 3. 싸요

### 9) My Sentence

How is your mood or condition today?

- 오늘 기분이 _______________아/어요.


---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_12.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**ㄹ/을 수 있어요 / 없어요.**
- *“Can [Verb] / Cannot [Verb].”*

### 2) Core Meaning

It’s used when talking about your ability or the possibility of a situation. It is very important when clearly expressing an objective intention, like saying “I can do that!” or on the contrary, “Sorry, I can’t do it right now because of the situation.”

### 3) Grammar

- Choose based on the Batchim of the Verb Stem.
- **When there is NO Batchim:** + ㄹ 수 있어요/없어요 (e.g., 하다 →**할 수 있어요**)
- **When there is a Batchim:** + 을 수 있어요/없어요 (e.g., 먹다 →**먹을 수 있어요**)
- **Exception (‘ㄹ’ Batchim):** Leave the ‘ㄹ’ Batchim as it is and just attach ‘수 있어요’. (e.g., 놀다 →**놀 수 있어요**)

### 4) How it’s used

- When talking about ability: “매운 거 먹을 수 있어요.”
- When asking about possibility: “내일 만날 수 있어요?”
- When politely declining: “죄송하지만, 갈 수 없어요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**하다** (Do),**가다** (Go),**먹다** (Eat),**만나다** (Meet),**읽다** (Read),**오다** (Come)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **할 수 있어요.** (I can do it.)
- **갈 수 있어요.** (I can go.)
- **먹을 수 있어요.** (I can eat it.)
- **만날 수 있어요.** (I can meet.)
- **올 수 있어요.** (I can come.)
- **할 수 없어요.** (I cannot do it.)
- **갈 수 없어요.** (I cannot go.)
- **먹을 수 없어요.** (I cannot eat it.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **매운 음식 먹을 수 있어요.** (I can eat spicy food.)
- **내일 만날 수 있어요?** (Can we meet tomorrow?)
- **한글 읽을 수 있어요.** (I can read Hangul.)
- **미안해요, 내일은 만날 수 없어요.** (Sorry, I cannot meet tomorrow.)
- **주말에 일할 수 있어요?** (Can you work on the weekend?)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Fill in the blanks (Express your ability)

1. (When speaking a foreign language) 저 한국어 _______________. (하다)
2. (When you have a driver’s license) 저 자동차 운전_______________. (하다)
3. (At a swimming pool) 물을 무서워해서 수영_______________. (하다 + 없어요)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 할 수 있어요 / 2. 할 수 있어요 / 3. 할 수 없어요

### 9) My Sentence

What is something you confidently ‘can do’ in Korean?

- 저는 _______________ㄹ/을 수 있어요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_13.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**ㄹ/을 줄 알아요 / 몰라요.**
- *“Know how to [Verb] / Don’t know how to [Verb].”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is a pattern used to say whether you know how to do something or have the technique to do it. It is perfect when talking about skills like driving, cooking, or swimming.

### 3) Grammar

- The rules to make this form are exactly the same as ‘ㄹ/을 수 있어요’ we just learned!
- **When there is NO Batchim:** + ㄹ 줄 알아요/몰라요 (e.g., 타다 →**탈 줄 알아요**)
- **When there is a Batchim:** + 을 줄 알아요/몰라요 (e.g., 읽다 →**읽을 줄 알아요**)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **CRITICAL FIX (Be careful!)**
This grammar indicates the ‘method’ of doing something, so it can**ONLY be attached to Verbs**. If you attach it to an adjective (e.g., 예쁠 줄 알았어요), it changes to a completely different meaning: “I thought it would be pretty (an incorrect assumption)”, so please be careful!
> 

### 4) How it’s used

- When talking about skills: “운전할 줄 알아요.”
- When talking about language ability: “한국말 할 줄 알아요?”
- When making food: “김치찌개 끓일 줄 알아요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**운전하다** (Drive),**수영하다** (Swim),**요리하다** (Cook),**타다** (Ride - bike/ski),**치다** (Play - piano)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **할 줄 알아요.** (I know how to do it.)
- **운전할 줄 알아요.** (I know how to drive.)
- **수영할 줄 알아요.** (I know how to swim.)
- **요리할 줄 알아요.** (I know how to cook.)
- **탈 줄 몰라요.** (I don’t know how to ride it.)
- **할 줄 몰라요.** (I don’t know how to do it.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **한국어 할 줄 알아요.** (I know how to speak Korean.)
- **자전거 탈 줄 몰라요.** (I don’t know how to ride a bicycle.)
- **김치찌개 끓일 줄 알아요.** (I know how to cook Kimchi stew.)
- **피아노 칠 줄 알아요.** (I know how to play the piano.)
- **한글 읽을 줄 알아요.** (I know how to read Hangul.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Fill in the blanks (Ask or answer about skills)

1. (At a ski resort) 스키 _______________? (타다 + 의문문)
2. (Instrument) 저는 기타를 _______________. (치다)
3. (Language) 저는 중국어 _______________. (하다 + 몰라요)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 탈 줄 알아요 / 2. 칠 줄 알아요 / 3. 할 줄 몰라요

### 9) My Sentence

What is a special skill or foreign language you know how to do?

- 저는 _______________ㄹ/을 줄 알아요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_14.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**아/어 봤어요.**
- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**ㄴ/은 적이 있어요.**
- *“Have tried [Verb]-ing / Have ever [Verb]-ed.”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is a magical pattern to use when showing off or sharing your special life experiences. You use it when talking about something you’ve done for the first time or a memorable past experience, like “I’ve been there!” or “I’ve tried eating that!”

### 3) Grammar

- There are two expressions. The meanings are similar, but the way they are formed is different.
- **아/어 봤어요 (A light attempt or experience):** Connect ‘아/어’ according to the vowel rules and attach ‘봤어요 (from 보다)’. (e.g., 가다 →**가 봤어요**)
- **ㄴ/은 적이 있어요 (A clear past fact):** Depending on the Batchim, use ‘-ㄴ 적이 있어요’ if there’s no Batchim, and ‘-은 적이 있어요’ if there is a Batchim. (e.g., 가다 →**간 적이 있어요**)

### 4) How it’s used

- When talking about travel experiences: “제주도에 가 봤어요.”
- When talking about food experiences: “산낙지 먹어 본 적 있어요?”
- When experiencing a culture: “한복 입어 봤어요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**가다** (Go),**먹다** (Eat),**입다** (Wear),**보다** (See/Watch),**해보다** (Try doing)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **가 봤어요.** (I have been there.)
- **간 적이 있어요.** (I have been there.)
- **먹어 봤어요.** (I have tried eating it.)
- **먹은 적이 있어요.** (I have eaten it.)
- **해 봤어요.** (I have tried doing it.)
- **본 적 있어요.** (I have seen it.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **제주도에 가 봤어요.** (I have been to Jeju Island.)
- **그 영화 본 적 있어요.** (I have seen that movie.)
- **한복 입어 본 적 있어요.** (I have tried wearing a Hanbok.)
- **한국 음식 먹어 봤어요.** (I have tried Korean food.)
- **연예인 만난 적이 있어요.** (I have met a celebrity.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Change to expressions of experience. (아/어 봤어요 form)

1. 산낙지를 ( 먹다 ) → _______________
2. 소주를 ( 마시다 ) → _______________
3. 한국에 ( 가다 ) → _______________
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 먹어 봤어요 / 2. 마셔 봤어요 / 3. 가 봤어요

### 9) My Sentence

What is a special experience you’ve had in your life?

- 저는 _______________에 가 본 적이 있어요.


---


![[survival_mission_2.png|700]]


![[chapter_03.png|700]]

![[sulsul_pattern_15.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 5W1H 의문사 Question Words ]** +**예요/이에요? or 해요?**
- *“Who/What/Where/When/Why/How is it? / do you do it?”*

### 2) Core Meaning

The most important thing when keeping a conversation going in Korean is throwing out questions! Use this pattern when you are curious about who, when, where, what, how, or why someone did something. Try starting a ping-pong conversation by asking the other person questions.

### 3) Grammar

Just attach your question right after the word corresponding to the 5W1H (Who, What, Where, When, Why, How).

- **누구 (nu-gu) / 누가 (nu-ga)** = Who
- **언제 (eon-je)** = When
- **어디 (eo-di)** = Where
- **무엇을 / 뭐 (mu-eo-seul / mwo)** = What
- **어떻게 (eo-tteo-ke)** = How
- **왜 (wae)** = Why

When asking with a noun, use ‘예요/이에요?’, and when asking about an action, use it together with a verb.
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip: ‘누구’ vs ‘누가’ (Crucial!)**
This is a mistake foreign students make all the time! The basic word for ‘Who’ is**‘누구’**.
> 
> - When asking with a noun: **누구 + 예요? = 누구예요? (O)** / 누가예요? (X)
> - When asking about a verb (action): When ‘누구’ meets the subject particle ‘가’, the shape changes to **‘누가(누구+가)’**. So, when asking about an action, you MUST say **“누가 했어요?”** (Who did it?)!

### 4) How it’s used

- When asking for personal info: “이름이 뭐예요?”, “저 사람 누구예요?”
- When asking about a destination or plan: “어디 가요?”, “언제 만날까요?”
- When asking for a reason: “왜 그래요?”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**이름** (Name),**직업** (Job),**전화번호** (Phone number),**이거** (This),**가다** (Go),**만나다** (Meet)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **이름이 뭐예요?** (What is your name?)
- **이거 뭐예요?** (What is this?)
- **저 사람 누구예요?** (Who is that person?)
- **누가 왔어요?** (Who came?)
- **언제 가요?** (When are you going?)
- **어디 가요?** (Where are you going?)
- **어떻게 해요?** (How do you do it?)
- **왜요?** (Why?)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **직업이 뭐예요?** (What is your job?)
- **전화번호가 뭐예요?** (What is your phone number?)
- **한국에 언제 왔어요?** (When did you come to Korea?)
- **왜 늦었어요?** (Why are you late?)
- **주말에 보통 뭐 해요?** (What do you usually do on weekends?)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Insert the correct question word. (누구, 누가, 언제, 왜)

1. (When asking who a person is) 저 사람은 _______________예요?
2. (When asking who did the action) 이 케이크 _______________ 먹었어요?
3. (When asking the time) 생일이 _______________예요?
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 누구 / 2. 누가 / 3. 언제

### 9) My Sentence

What do you want to ask your newly-made Korean friend?

- 저기요, _______________ _______________?

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_16.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 명사 Noun ]** +**은/는요?**
- *“How about [Noun]?” / “And [Noun]?”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Masters of conversation don’t just talk about themselves. They gently pass the ball back to the other person! This is a magical expression used when you finish talking about yourself and ask back, “How about you?”, or when you want to smoothly change the topic to another person or object.

### 3) Grammar

Attach 은/는 after the noun and raise the end with ‘요?’.

- **When there is a Batchim:** + 은요? (e.g., 선생님 →**선생님은요?**)
- **When there is NO Batchim:** + 는요? (e.g., 커피 →**커피는요?**)

### 4) How it’s used

- When asking for the other’s opinion after answering a question: “저는 커피요. 지민 씨는요?”
- When asking about another object: “김치는 있어요. 그럼 밥은요?”
- When returning a greeting: “전 잘 지내요. 선생님은요?”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**이거** (This),**저거** (That),**오늘** (Today),**내일** (Tomorrow),**주말** (Weekend)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **마이클 씨는요?** (How about you, Michael?)
- **선생님은요?** (How about you, teacher?)
- **이거는요?** (How about this one?)
- **저거는요?** (How about that one?)
- **오늘은요?** (How about today?)
- **내일은요?** (How about tomorrow?)
- **가격은요?** (How about the price?)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **저는 학생이에요. 지민 씨는요?** (I am a student. How about you, Jimin?)
- **저는 피자 먹을래요. 마크 씨는요?** (I’ll have pizza. How about you, Mark?)
- **이건 매워요. 저건요?** (This one is spicy. What about that one?)
- **어제는 바빴어요. 오늘은요?** (I was busy yesterday. How about today?)
- **비빔밥 하나 주세요. 아, 음료수는요?** (One Bibimbap, please. Ah, how about drinks?)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Try asking the other person back.

1. A: 저는 미국 사람이에요.
B: 아, 그래요? (How about you?) _______________?
2. A: 일요일에는 시간이 안 돼요.
B: 그럼 (How about Saturday?) _______________?
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. (이름 Name) 씨는요? / 2. 토요일은요?

### 9) My Sentence

State your situation and ask back about the other person’s situation.

- 저는 _______________. ( 친구 이름 Friend’s Name ) 씨는요?

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_17.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **진짜요? / 정말요?** (Surprise)
- **그래요?** (Neutral / Interest)
- **대박! / 헐!** (Exclamation - Slang)

### 2) Core Meaning

These are the most essential reactions used when listening to someone! Just by using these two phrases well when you’re surprised, amazed, or intrigued, the other person will feel like you deeply empathize with their story. 50% of Korean conversation is reaction!

### 3) Grammar

These are independent words, so you can just throw them out without worrying about grammar. Intonation is the most important part!

- **진짜요? ↗:** Raising the end shows ‘surprise’ or ‘curiosity’.
- **진짜요… ↘:** Dropping the end slightly shows ‘disappointment’ or ‘deep empathy’.

### 4) How it’s used

- When hearing unexpected news: “저 내일 결혼해요!” → “진짜요?!”
- When learning a new fact: “이 식당 매주 월요일에 쉬어요.” → “아, 그래요?”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**진짜요** (Really?),**정말요** (Really?),**그래요** (Is that so?),**대박** (Awesome/Wow),**헐** (OMG)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **진짜요?** (Really? / Seriously?)
- **정말요?** (Really? - slightly more formal)
- **그래요?** (Is that so? / I didn’t know that.)
- **대박!** (Daebak! / Wow! / Awesome! - among close friends)
- **헐!** (Heol! / OMG! / No way! - among close friends)
- **우와!** (Wow!)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- A: **저 내일 한국 가요.**
B: **진짜요?! 축하해요!** (Really?! Congratulations!)
- A: **이 식당 진짜 맛있어요.**
B: **아, 그래요? 저도 가보고 싶어요.** (Oh, is that so? I want to go there too.)
- A: **저 어제 복권 당첨됐어요!**
B: **대박! 진짜 대박이다.** (Awesome! That is totally crazy.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Native Skill)**
Try attaching ‘아 (Ah)’ in front of ‘진짜요’ and say**“아, 진짜요?”**. It makes the reaction sound much more natural and rich. It’s a must-equip reaction for Koreans!
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Choose the reaction that fits the situation.

1. When a friend says sadly, “나 어제 지갑 잃어버렸어…” (I lost my wallet yesterday…):
( 아, 그래요? ↗ / 진짜요… ↘ )
2. When a friend says, “나 방탄소년단 콘서트 티켓 구했어!” (I got BTS concert tickets!):
( 대박! / 그렇구나. )
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 진짜요… ↘ / 2. 대박!

### 9) My Sentence

Pick the one reaction that sticks to your mouth the best.

- My ultimate #1 reaction: “_______________!”

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_18.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **맞아요** = *“That’s right”, “Correct”*
- **아니에요** = *“That’s not it”, “No, thanks”, “You’re welcome”*

### 2) Core Meaning

You use “맞아요!” when strongly agreeing that what the other person said is correct, and “아니에요~” when they are wrong, when politely declining, or when humbly responding to a compliment. These expressions act like a compass that firmly sets the direction of the conversation.

### 3) Grammar

These expressions come from ‘맞다 (to be correct)’ and ‘아니다 (to not be)’. They are used independently or placed at the very beginning of a sentence.

### 4) How it’s used

- When agreeing with someone’s opinion: “오늘 너무 춥죠?” → “네, 맞아요!”
- When correcting a misunderstanding: “미국 사람이에요?” → “아니에요, 캐나다 사람이에요.”
- When humbly replying to a compliment: “한국말 잘하시네요!” → “에이, 아니에요~”
- When replying to ‘thank you’: “감사합니다!” → “아니에요~ (You’re welcome)”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**네 맞아요** (Yes that’s right),**아니에요** (No it’s not)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **맞아요.** (That’s right.)
- **네, 맞아요.** (Yes, that’s correct.)
- **아니에요.** (No, that’s not it / You’re welcome.)
- **아닙니다.** (No, that’s not it - a bit more formal)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- A: **이거 제이콥 씨 가방이에요?**
B: **네, 맞아요.** (Yes, that’s right.)
- A: **오늘 날씨가 참 춥네요.**
B: **맞아요, 진짜 춥네요.** (That’s right, it’s really cold.)
- A: **피곤해 보여요.**
B: **아니에요, 괜찮아요.** (No, I’m okay.)
- A: **한국어 정말 잘하시네요!**
B: **에이, 아니에요.** (Oh, no not at all.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Choose the appropriate reaction.

1. (Reply when someone opens the door for you and says “감사합니다!”): _______________.
2. (Reply to the waiter asking “비빔밥 시키셨죠?”): 네, _______________.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 아니에요 (or 괜찮아요) / 2. 맞아요

### 9) My Sentence

Imagine someone gave you a huge compliment. Respond to it humbly.

- 에이, _______________아/어요. 아직 멀었어요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_19.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **그렇구나 / 그렇네요**
- *“I see”, “Ah, that makes sense”, “Indeed”*

### 2) Core Meaning

You use this when you learn a new fact and nod saying “아~ 그렇구나” (Ah~ I see), or when you empathize with someone’s words and say “진짜 그렇네요” (It really is). If ‘진짜요?’ is for surprise, this one is for ‘warm empathy’ and ‘realization’.

### 3) Grammar

- **그렇구나:** Used when lightly expressing realization like talking to yourself, or when speaking casually (banmal) with close friends.
- **그렇네요:** Used politely to chime in and agree with the other person.

### 4) How it’s used

- When you just learned something you didn’t know: “저는 매운 거 못 먹어요.” → “아, 그렇구나.”
- When acknowledging a situation while agreeing with the other person: “벌써 시간이 이렇게 됐네요.” → “아, 그렇네요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**그렇구나** (I see),**그렇네요** (Indeed),**아** (Ah)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **그렇구나.** (I see.)
- **아~ 그렇구나.** (Ah, I see.)
- **그렇네요.** (Indeed / That makes sense.)
- **아, 정말 그렇네요.** (Ah, it really is.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- A: **저는 강아지 알레르기가 있어요.**
B: **아, 그렇구나. 몰랐어요.** (Ah, I see. I didn’t know.)
- A: **비가 와서 그런지 기분이 꿀꿀해요.**
B: **아, 그렇구나…** (Ah, I see…)
- A: **이 식당은 김치가 진짜 맛있어요.**
B: **맞아요, 먹어보니까 진짜 그렇네요.** (You’re right, having tasted it, it really is.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Pronunciation!)**
When pronouncing ‘그렇네요’, you must smoothly nasalize it as**[그런네요 / geu-reon-ne-yo]** to sound natural like a native speaker!
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Provide an empathetic reaction that fits the situation.

1. (When learning a new fact, as if talking to yourself) 아, _______________.
2. (Agreeing with the teacher’s explanation) 아, 정말 _______________.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 그렇구나 / 2. 그렇네요

### 9) My Sentence

The other person just explained a Korean culture you didn’t know about. How would you react?

- 아, _______________. 저는 몰랐어요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_20.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **무슨 뜻이에요? / 무슨 말이에요?**
- *“What does that mean?” / “What are you talking about?”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is a very useful ‘life jacket’ question when you hear a word you don’t know, or when you can’t figure out the intention behind what the other person is saying. When conversing in Korean, you will inevitably hear words you don’t know, so don’t hesitate to throw out this question!

### 3) Grammar

- **무슨 (mu-seun)** = What kind of
- **뜻 (tteut)** = Meaning
- **말 (mal)** = Word / Saying
Just attach it right after the unknown word to say**“[ Unknown Word ] + 무슨 뜻이에요?”** and it’s perfect.

### 4) How it’s used

- When asking the meaning of a word: “대박이 무슨 뜻이에요?”
- When you don’t understand the other’s intent or situation: “갑자기 취소라니, 무슨 말이에요?”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**뜻** (Meaning),**말** (Word/Saying),**무슨** (What kind of)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **무슨 뜻이에요?** (What does it mean?)
- **그게 무슨 뜻이에요?** (What does that mean?)
- **무슨 말이에요?** (What do you mean by that?)
- **그게 무슨 말이에요?** (What are you talking about?)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **‘대박’이 무슨 뜻이에요?** (What does ’Daebak’ mean?)
- **‘가성비’가 무슨 뜻이에요?** (What does ’Gaseongbi’ mean?)
- **잘 이해가 안 돼요. 무슨 말이에요?** (I don’t really understand. What do you mean?)
- **무슨 뜻인지 잘 모르겠어요.** (I don’t really know what that means.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Create a question asking for the meaning.

1. (Your Korean friend said ‘헐’. Ask what this means.)
→ ’헐’이 _______________?
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 무슨 뜻이에요

### 9) My Sentence

Insert a Korean word you heard recently and were curious about, and ask the question.

- _______________이/가 무슨 뜻이에요?

## 💎 Special Unit 2. 한국인의 호칭 정리 (Family & Titles)

“저기요” (Excuse me), “사장님” (Boss), “언니” (Older sister)

The thing foreigners find most confusing is ‘titles’. In English, you can just call someone by their name or use ‘You’ if they aren’t family. But in Korea, people call even complete strangers “언니 (older sister)” or “사장님 (boss/owner)”. Shall we learn how to call people naturally, just like Koreans who value relationships so much?

**1. 가족 같은 사이 (Social Family Terms)**

When you become close, Koreans use family titles even if you don’t share a drop of blood!

- **When you are a Female:** An older male =**오빠 (Oppa)** / An older female =**언니 (Unnie)**
- **When you are a Male:** An older male =**형 (Hyung)** / An older female =**누나 (Noona)**
- **Someone younger than you:** Regardless of gender, it’s**동생 (Dongsaeng)** (But you usually just call them by their name!)

**2. 식당과 가게에서 (In Stores & Restaurants)**

- **사장님 (Owner/Boss):** This is the safest and most pleasant title to use when respectfully calling someone you don’t know, like a store owner, staff member, or a taxi driver. (“사장님, 이거 얼마예요?”)
- **저기요 (Excuse me):** When the title is ambiguous, this is the most common expression used to get someone’s attention. (“저기요, 주문할게요.”)

**3. 절대 쓰면 안 되는 호칭 (Warning: Never Use These)**

- **당신 (You):** In the dictionary, it translates to ‘You’, but if you say “당신” to a stranger, it can sound aggressive, like you’re picking a fight.
- **너 (You):** Unless you are 100% sure they are the same age or younger, never casually use “너”.

**4. Practice**

✔**Practice 1.** Find the correct title.

1. (In a taxi) _______________, 강남역으로 가주세요.
2. (When you, a female, are calling your older male friend ‘Minsu’) 민수 _______________
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 사장님 (or 기사님) / 2. 오빠

---


---


---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_21.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **잘 알겠어요 / 잘 모르겠어요**
- *“I understand well” / “I don’t really understand (know)”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is an expression to clearly, yet very politely, let the other person know whether you understood what they said or not. Instead of just saying “알아요/몰라요” (I know/don’t know), saying “알겠어요/모르겠어요” is a much softer and more natural native expression. Adding ‘잘’ (well) makes it even more natural.

### 3) Grammar

It’s formed by attaching ‘-겠어요’ to the verbs ‘알다 (to know)’ and ‘모르다 (to not know)’.
Here, ‘-겠-’ doesn’t indicate the future; it serves to gently express ‘my state or guess’.

### 4) How it’s used

- When you understood after hearing an explanation: “아, 이제 잘 알겠어요.”
- When someone asks for directions but you don’t know: “죄송해요, 저도 잘 모르겠어요.”
- When you haven’t fully understood: “아직 잘 모르겠어요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**알겠어요** (I understand),**모르겠어요** (I don’t know/understand),**잘** (Well),**아직** (Yet),**이제** (Now)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **알겠어요.** (I understand.)
- **잘 알겠어요.** (I understand well.)
- **모르겠어요.** (I don’t know/understand.)
- **잘 모르겠어요.** (I don’t really know/understand.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **네, 이제 잘 알겠어요.** (Yes, I understand well now.)
- **죄송해요, 아직 잘 모르겠어요.** (I’m sorry, I don’t really understand yet.)
- **저 사람은 누군지 잘 모르겠어요.** (I don’t really know who that person is.)
- **길을 잘 모르겠어요.** (I don’t really know the way.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Native Skill)**
When someone asks for directions, instead of flatly saying “몰라요!” (I don’t know!), answering with “잘 모르겠어요~” gives off a much more polite and apologetically kind nuance.
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Choose the right phrase.

1. (When the tutor explains grammar and you perfectly understand): 네, _______________.
2. (When a Korean asks for directions too fast and you can’t answer): 죄송해요, 저도 _______________.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. (잘) 알겠어요 / 2. 잘 모르겠어요

### 9) My Sentence

When you hear something difficult, practice politely saying that you didn’t understand.

- 죄송하지만, _______________.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_22.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **다시 말해 주세요 / 천천히 말해 주세요**
- *“Please say it again” / “Please speak slowly”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is a must-have life jacket expression you can pull out when the other person speaks too fast or when you couldn’t catch what they said! Koreans are happy to adjust their speed when talking to foreigners, so don’t panic and make sure to use this pattern to get another chance.

### 3) Grammar

It’s structured as Adverb + 말해 주세요 (말하다 + 아/어 주세요).

- **다시 (da-si)** = Again
- **천천히 (cheon-cheon-hi)** = Slowly

### 4) How it’s used

- When it’s too noisy around and you couldn’t hear.
- When your Korean friend’s speech is too fast, like a rap.
- When you need to double-check an important appointment time or number.

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**다시** (Again),**천천히** (Slowly),**한 번 더** (One more time),**조금만** (A little bit)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **다시 말해 주세요.** (Please say it again.)
- **천천히 말해 주세요.** (Please speak slowly.)
- **한 번 더 말해 주세요.** (Please say it one more time.)
- **다시 해 주세요.** (Please do it again.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **죄송하지만 다시 말해 주세요.** (I’m sorry, but please say it again.)
- **너무 빨라요. 조금 천천히 말해 주세요.** (It’s too fast. Please speak a little slowly.)
- **한 번 더 다시 말해 주시겠어요?** (Could you please say it one more time?)
- **잘 못 들었어요. 다시 말해 주세요.** (I couldn’t hear you well. Please say it again.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Native Skill)**
If you attach**‘죄송하지만 (joe-song-ha-ji-man / I’m sorry but)’** to the front of the sentence, it brings out a 200% polite vibe. The other person will gladly explain it again with a smile!
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Ask for help according to the situation.

1. (When the Korean listening test is too fast): 조금만 _______________ 말해 주세요.
2. (When you couldn’t write down the other person’s phone number): 죄송한데 _______________ 말해 주세요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 천천히 / 2. 다시 (or 한 번 더)

### 9) My Sentence

Write down your own ‘SOS’ phrase to use when you get stuck conversing with a Korean.

- _______________지만, _______________.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_23.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**아/어 주세요 / 주시겠어요?**
- *“Please do [Verb] for me” / “Could you please do [Verb] for me?”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Unlike ‘주세요 (Give me)’ which attaches to a noun, this attaches **after a verb to politely ask someone to perform an action**. It is very useful when asking someone to ‘do’ something for you, like opening a door or taking a picture!

### 3) Grammar

The shape changes depending on the last vowel of the verb! (The rules are identical to the past tense)

- **When it is ㅏ, ㅗ:** + 아 주세요 (e.g., 닫다 →**닫아 주세요**)
- **For all other vowels:** + 어 주세요 (e.g., 열다 →**열어 주세요**, 찍다 →**찍어 주세요**)
- **‘하다’ verbs:** + 해 주세요 (e.g., 추천하다 →**추천해 주세요**)

Using ‘-주시겠어요?’ makes the request slightly more formal and cautious, like “Could you please…?”.

### 4) How it’s used

- When asking for physical help: “문 좀 열어 주세요.”
- When requesting a service: “이거 포장해 주세요.”
- When asking for a favor at a tourist spot: “사진 좀 찍어 주세요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**열다** (Open),**닫다** (Close),**찍다** (Take a picture),**포장하다** (Pack/To-go),**추천하다** (Recommend),**쓰다** (Write)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **열어 주세요.** (Please open it.)
- **닫아 주세요.** (Please close it.)
- **찍어 주세요.** (Please take a picture.)
- **포장해 주세요.** (Please pack this to-go.)
- **추천해 주세요.** (Please recommend something.)
- **써 주세요.** (Please write it down.)
- **확인해 주세요.** (Please check it.)
- **도와주세요.** (Please help me.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **사진 좀 찍어 주세요.** (Please take a picture for me.)
- **창문 좀 닫아 주시겠어요?** (Could you please close the window?)
- **맛있는 메뉴로 추천해 주세요.** (Please recommend a delicious menu item.)
- **여기에 이름 써 주세요.** (Please write your name here.)
- **이거 포장해 주시겠어요?** (Could you please pack this for me?)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Magic Cushion Word)**
When making a request, placing**‘좀 (jom / a little, please)’** in front of the verb makes it sound much softer. Koreans use ‘좀’ habitually like breathing when asking for a favor! (Rather than “창문 열어 주세요”, use “창문 **좀** 열어 주세요”)
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Change to a polite request.

1. (Take a picture) 사진 좀 _______________.
2. (Show the menu) 메뉴판 좀 보여 _______________? (Formally)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 찍어 주세요 / 2. 주시겠어요

### 9) My Sentence

Try asking a restaurant or hotel staff to do an action for you.

- 저기요, 이것 좀 _______________아/어 주세요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_24.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 형용사 / 동사 어간 Adjective/Verb Stem ]** +**지 않아요?**
- *“Isn’t it [Adjective]?” / “Doesn’t it [Verb]?”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is a stylish expression used to casually ask the other person if they agree with your thought. Instead of asking “이거 예쁘죠?” (This is pretty, right?), asking “이거 예쁘지 않아요?” (Isn’t this pretty?) brings out a much more native and natural nuance!

### 3) Grammar

Regardless of whether the Adjective or Verb Stem has a Batchim or not, just drop ‘다’ from the dictionary form and attach ‘-지 않아요?’ right away.

- 예쁘다 → **예쁘지 않아요?**
- 맛있다 →**맛있지 않아요?**
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Be extremely careful with pronunciation!)**
Foreign students often get confused with the pronunciation of this pattern!
‘-지 않아요?’ must be pronounced smoothly as**[지 아나요 / ji a-na-yo]**.
If you pronounce it as**[자나요(잖아요) / ja-na-yo]**, it turns into a nagging or annoyed meaning like “Come on, you know it’s pretty!”, so you must be very careful!
> 
> - 예쁘 **지 않아요?** [예쁘지 아나요] = Isn’t it pretty? (Asking for agreement)
> - 예쁘 **잖아요!** [예쁘자나요] = Come on, you know it’s pretty! (Nagging/Emphasizing)

### 4) How it’s used

- When seeking agreement on your taste while looking at an item: “이 옷 예쁘지 않아요?”
- When wanting to empathize about the weather or situation: “오늘 너무 춥지 않아요?”
- When bringing up your opinion cautiously: “그거 좀 이상하지 않아요?”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**예쁘다** (Pretty),**춥다** (Cold),**덥다** (Hot),**맛있다** (Delicious),**비싸다** (Expensive),**이상하다** (Weird)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **예쁘지 않아요?** (Isn’t it pretty?)
- **맛있지 않아요?** (Isn’t it delicious?)
- **비싸지 않아요?** (Isn’t it expensive?)
- **춥지 않아요?** (Isn’t it cold?)
- **덥지 않아요?** (Isn’t it hot?)
- **이상하지 않아요?** (Isn’t it weird?)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **이 옷 예쁘지 않아요?** (Aren’t these clothes pretty?)
- **오늘 날씨 너무 춥지 않아요?** (Isn’t the weather too cold today?)
- **그 영화 좀 재미없지 않아요?** (Isn’t that movie a bit boring?)
- **이 식당 진짜 맛있지 않아요?** (Isn’t this restaurant really delicious?)
- **이거 좀 비싸지 않아요?** (Isn’t this a bit expensive?)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Ask the other person for their agreement.

1. (While eating spicy Tteokbokki) 이거 너무 _______________? (맵다)
2. (Looking at an expensive bag) 이 가방 예쁜데, 좀 _______________? (비싸다)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 맵지 않아요 / 2. 비싸지 않아요

### 9) My Sentence

Show something you like to your friend and ask for their agreement.

- 이거 정말 _______________지 않아요?

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_25.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **같이** +**[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**할래요? / ㄹ/을까요?**
- *“Do you want to [Verb] together?” / “Shall we [Verb] together?”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is a warm expression used when you sweetly suggest doing something together with a friend or coworker. It is the absolute perfect way to ask someone out on a date or for a meal without putting pressure on them, while respecting their wishes!

### 3) Grammar

Choose based on whether the Verb Stem has a Batchim or not.

- **When there is NO Batchim:** + ㄹ래요? / ㄹ까요? (e.g., 보다 →**볼래요?**,**볼까요?**)
- **When there is a Batchim:** + 을래요? / 을까요? (e.g., 먹다 →**먹을래요?**,**먹을까요?**)
- **ㄹ래요?:** ‘Do you want to~?’ Used more casually between close people to directly ask the other person’s intention.
- **ㄹ까요?:** ‘Shall we~?’ It strongly feels like asking for the other person’s opinion, so it gives a slightly more cautious and polite feeling.

### 4) How it’s used

- When suggesting a meal: “오늘 점심 같이 먹을까요?”
- When asking someone on a date: “주말에 영화 볼래요?”
- When offering something: “커피 한잔할래요?”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**같이** (Together),**보다** (Watch/See),**먹다** (Eat),**가다** (Go),**커피 한잔하다** (Grab a cup of coffee)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **같이 볼래요?** (Do you want to watch it together?)
- **같이 볼까요?** (Shall we watch it together?)
- **같이 먹을래요?** (Do you want to eat together?)
- **같이 먹을까요?** (Shall we eat together?)
- **같이 갈래요?** (Do you want to go together?)
- **같이 갈까요?** (Shall we go together?)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **주말에 같이 영화 볼래요?** (Do you want to watch a movie together on the weekend?)
- **오늘 점심 같이 먹을까요?** (Shall we eat lunch together today?)
- **수업 끝나고 같이 커피 한잔할래요?** (Do you want to grab a cup of coffee together after class?)
- **내일 같이 도서관에 갈까요?** (Shall we go to the library together tomorrow?)
- **저녁에 같이 술 한잔할까요?** (Shall we grab a drink together tonight?)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Magic Seasoning)**
When making a suggestion, make sure to put**‘같이 (ga-chi)’** in front of the sentence. It is pronounced as [가치]. Just adding this single word ‘같이’ doubles the warmth!
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Make a gentle suggestion.

1. (To a coworker at lunchtime) 오늘 밥 같이 _______________? (먹다 / ㄹ까요)
2. (Asking out someone you’re interested in on a date) 주말에 같이 영화 _______________? (보다 / ㄹ래요)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 먹을까요 / 2. 볼래요

### 9) My Sentence

Try suggesting doing something together to your Korean friend!

- _______________에 저랑 같이 _______________ㄹ/을까요?

---




![[survival_mission_3.png|700]]


![[chapter_04.png|700]]

![[sulsul_pattern_26.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 명사 Noun ]** +**에서** +**[ 동사 Verb ]**
- *“[Verb] at/in [Noun]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is the magic word used when an action or event happens at a specific place. If ‘에(e)’ simply indicates the location of existence or your destination, ‘에서(e-seo)’ shows that the place is the actual ‘stage’ where the action unfolds!

### 3) Grammar

- Attach ‘에서’ right after the noun representing the place. It’s always ‘에서’ regardless of whether there is a Batchim or not.
- After ‘에서’, verbs representing actions (like studying, eating, meeting) usually follow.

### 4) How it’s used

- When talking about your daily routine: “카페에서 공부해요.”
- When setting a meeting place: “어디에서 만날까요?”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**집** (Home),**식당** (Restaurant),**카페** (Cafe),**학교** (School),**회사** (Office),**도서관** (Library),**한국** (Korea)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **집에서** (At home)
- **식당에서** (At the restaurant)
- **카페에서** (At the cafe)
- **학교에서** (At school)
- **회사에서** (At the office)
- **어디에서** (Where at)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **카페에서 공부해요.** (I study at a cafe.)
- **식당에서 밥을 먹었어요.** (I ate at a restaurant.)
- **회사에서 일해요.** (I work at the office.)
- **주말에 공원에서 산책해요.** (I take a walk in the park on weekends.)
- **우리 내일 어디에서 만날까요?** (Where shall we meet tomorrow?)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Native Skill)**
> 
> 
> In daily conversation, people often shorten ‘어디에서’ and say **‘어디서 (eo-di-seo)’**! Try saying “어디서 만날까요?”. It sounds much more natural.
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Insert the correct place particle. (에 vs 에서)

1. (Presence) 저는 지금 집_______________ 있어요.
2. (Action) 저는 지금 집_______________ 넷플릭스를 봐요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 에 / 2. 에서

### 9) My Sentence

Write down the place where you usually spend your time and what you do there.

- 저는 주로 _______________에서 _______________해요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_27.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 명사 Noun 1 ]** +**부터** +**[ 명사 Noun 2 ]** +**까지**
- *“From [Noun 1] to [Noun 2]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is a pattern used to express the starting and ending points of an event. By saying ‘from A to B’, you can neatly show the entire range of time or space all at once. This is an expression Koreans use dozens of times a day when talking about schedules or distances!

### 3) Grammar

- Attach it after nouns. You just attach ‘부터’ to the starting point and ‘까지’ to the ending point.
- It can be used for both time (1시부터 2시까지) and space/locations (서울부터 부산까지).

### 4) How it’s used

- When talking about business hours or schedules: “9시부터 6시까지 일해요.”
- When explaining a period: “월요일부터 금요일까지 바빠요.”
- When expressing distance: “여기부터 저기까지”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**아침** (Morning),**저녁** (Evening),**오늘** (Today),**내일** (Tomorrow),**월요일** (Monday),**금요일** (Friday),**여기** (Here),**저기** (There)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **1시부터 2시까지** (From 1 to 2)
- **여기부터 저기까지** (From here to there)
- **오늘부터 내일까지** (From today until tomorrow)
- **집부터 회사까지** (From home to work)
- **아침부터 저녁까지** (From morning to evening)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **아침부터 저녁까지 일했어요.** (I worked from morning until evening.)
- **1시부터 3시까지 회의가 있어요.** (I have a meeting from 1 o’clock to 3 o’clock.)
- **월요일부터 금요일까지 바빠요.** (I am busy from Monday to Friday.)
- **여기부터 저기까지 다 주세요.** (Please give me everything from here to there.)
- **어제부터 비가 와요.** (It has been raining since yesterday.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Fill in the blanks to create a range.

1. 저는 아침 9시_______________ 오후 6시_______________ 일해요.
2. (Talking about a vacation period) 다음 주 월요일_______________ 수요일_______________ 쉴 거예요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 부터, 까지 / 2. 부터, 까지

### 9) My Sentence

Express your working/studying hours or a certain period.

- 저는 _______________부터 _______________까지 바빠요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_28.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 시간 명사 Time Noun ]** +**에** +**[ 동사 Verb ]**
- *“[Verb] at/on/in [Time]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is an expression that pinpoints exactly ‘when’ something happens. By attaching ‘에’ after a time or day of the week, our appointment time or schedule becomes very clear. It’s an absolute must-have pattern when making plans!

### 3) Grammar

- Attach ‘에’ after a noun representing time.
- **CAUTION:** Do**NOT** attach ‘에’ after ‘오늘(today)’, ‘내일(tomorrow)’, ‘어제(yesterday)’, ‘지금(now)’, or ‘언제(when)’! (e.g., 내일에 만나요 X → 내일 만나요 O)

### 4) How it’s used

- When setting an appointment time: “2시에 만나요.”
- When explaining a daily routine: “아침에 운동해요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**시** (Hour),**분** (Minute),**아침** (Morning),**점심** (Noon),**저녁** (Evening),**주말** (Weekend)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **1시에** (At 1 o’clock)
- **아침에** (In the morning)
- **저녁에** (In the evening)
- **주말에** (On the weekend)
- **내년에** (Next year)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **주말에 영화를 봐요.** (I watch a movie on the weekend.)
- **2시에 만나요.** (Let’s meet at 2 o’clock.)
- **아침에 커피를 마셔요.** (I drink coffee in the morning.)
- **내년에 한국에 갈 거예요.** (I will go to Korea next year.)
- **오후 3시 반에 회의가 있어요.** (I have a meeting at 3:30 PM.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Insert the particle according to the time word.

1. (Monday) 월요일_______________ 만나요.
2. (Tomorrow) 내일_______________ 시간 있어요? (Be careful!)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 에 / 2. X (Do not use a particle)

### 9) My Sentence

What time do you usually wake up?

- 저는 매일 아침 _______________에 일어나요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_29.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 명사 Noun 1 ]** +**하고/랑** +**[ 명사 Noun 2 ]**
- *“[Noun 1] and/with [Noun 2]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

It acts as an ‘and’ connecting words nicely. Or it can be used when saying you do something ‘with’ someone. It is an incredibly common connecting link used in everyday life.

### 3) Grammar

- **하고 (ha-go):** Always use ‘하고’ regardless of Batchim. It gives a slightly cleaner feel.
- **랑/이랑 (rang/i-rang):** Gives a very friendly and casual feel. Use ‘랑’ if there is no Batchim, and ‘이랑’ if there is a Batchim. (e.g., 커피랑 / 수박이랑)

### 4) How it’s used

- When ordering multiple items: “이거랑 이거 주세요.”
- When stating who you are going with: “친구랑 가요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**친구** (Friend),**가족** (Family),**나** (Me),**너** (You),**물** (Water),**커피** (Coffee)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **물하고 커피** (Water and coffee)
- **밥하고 김치** (Rice and Kimchi)
- **친구랑** (With a friend)
- **엄마랑** (With Mom)
- **이거랑 저거** (This one and that one)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **빵하고 우유를 샀어요.** (I bought bread and milk.)
- **주말에 친구랑 영화를 봤어요.** (I watched a movie with a friend on the weekend.)
- **고양이하고 강아지를 좋아해요.** (I like cats and dogs.)
- **엄마랑 같이 밥을 먹었어요.** (I ate with my mom.)
- **김밥이랑 라면 주세요.** (Please give me Gimbap and Ramen.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Native Skill)**
> 
> 
> Memorizing the phrase **‘누구랑 같이 (nu-gu-rang ga-chi / together with whom)’** as a whole chunk is super useful! It’s used all the time in sentences like “누구랑 같이 왔어요?” (Who did you come with?).
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Connect the nouns.

1. (Coffee and cake) 커피_______________ 케이크 주세요. (Casually)
2. (You and me) 너_______________ 나.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 랑 / 2. 랑 (or 하고)

### 9) My Sentence

Who do you want to spend time with this weekend?

- 저는 이번 주말에 _______________랑 같이 놀고 싶어요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_30.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 명사 Noun 1 ]이/가** +**[ 명사 Noun 2 ]** +**보다 (더)** +**[ 형용사 Adjective ]**
- *“[Noun 1] is more [Adjective] than [Noun 2]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is an expression used when comparing two things. It’s an essential pattern when you want to firmly express your preference or make a choice, like saying “This is better than that!”. It’s a magic formula Koreans use every single day when shopping or choosing a menu!

### 3) Grammar

- Attach ‘보다’ behind **Noun 2 (the standard of comparison)**.
- An Adjective describing the state (크다, 좋다, 맛있다, etc.) follows.
- ‘더 (more)’ can be omitted, but using it together makes the feeling of comparison much stronger.

### 4) How it’s used

- When picking an item: “이게 저것보다 예뻐요.”
- When stating a preference: “저는 커피보다 차를 좋아해요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**더** (More),**덜** (Less),**좋다** (Good),**맛있다** (Delicious),**비싸다** (Expensive),**싸다** (Cheap),**크다** (Big)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **이것보다 예뻐요.** (It’s prettier than this.)
- **저것보다 싸요.** (It’s cheaper than that.)
- **사과보다 맛있어요.** (It’s more delicious than an apple.)
- **어제보다 좋아요.** (It’s better than yesterday.)
- **버스보다 빨라요.** (It’s faster than the bus.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **한국어가 영어보다 더 어려워요.** (Korean is more difficult than English.)
- **오늘이 어제보다 따뜻해요.** (Today is warmer than yesterday.)
- **치킨이 피자보다 맛있어요.** (Chicken is tastier than pizza.)
- **지하철이 버스보다 더 빨라요.** (The subway is faster than the bus.)
- **이 가방이 저 가방보다 비싸요.** (This bag is more expensive than that bag.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Insert the object of comparison.

1. (Comparing seasons) 저는 겨울_______________ 여름이 더 좋아요.
2. (Transportation) 택시_______________ 지하철이 더 빨라요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 보다 / 2. 보다

### 9) My Sentence

Try comparing two foods or items.

- _______________이/가 _______________보다 더 좋아요.

## 

---

 


## 

![[sulsul_pattern_31.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **제일 / 가장** +**[ 형용사/동사 Adjective/Verb ]**
- *“The most [Adjective] / [Verb] the most”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Use this when picking the #1 out of many! Slapping ‘제일’ or ‘가장’ right in front of an adjective or verb turns it into meaning “This is the best!”. There is no better word to use when saying what you like the most or where the most delicious food is.

### 3) Grammar

- Use it right in front of an adjective, an adverb, or verbs like ‘좋아하다/싫어하다’.
- ‘제일’ and ‘가장’ have exactly 100% the same meaning. In conversations, **‘제일’** is used a bit more casually and frequently.

### 4) How it’s used

- When giving the highest compliment: “이게 제일 예뻐요.”
- When stating your taste: “여름을 제일 좋아해요.”
- When asking for recommendations: “여기서 뭐가 제일 맛있어요?”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**제일** (The most),**가장** (The most),**좋다** (Good),**좋아하다** (Like),**맛있다** (Delicious),**비싸다** (Expensive)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **제일 좋아요.** (It’s the best.)
- **제일 예뻐요.** (It’s the prettiest.)
- **제일 커요.** (It’s the biggest.)
- **제일 맛있어요.** (It’s the most delicious.)
- **제일 비싸요.** (It’s the most expensive.)
- **가장 좋아요.** (It’s the best.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **이게 제일 맛있어요.** (This is the most delicious.)
- **한국에서 제주도가 가장 예뻐요.** (In Korea, Jeju Island is the most beautiful.)
- **무슨 영화를 제일 좋아해요?** (What movie do you like the most?)
- **우리 엄마가 세상에서 제일 좋아요.** (I like my mom the most in the world.)
- **여기서 제일 싼 거 주세요.** (Please give me the cheapest one here.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Create superlative expressions.

1. (Among fruits, I like strawberries the most) 과일 중에서 딸기를 _______________ 좋아해요.
2. (This dress is the prettiest) 이 원피스가 _______________ 예뻐요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 제일 (or 가장) / 2. 제일 (or 가장)

### 9) My Sentence

What is ‘the best’ in your personal taste?

- 저는 _______________을/를 제일 좋아해요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_32.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 명사 Noun ]** +**처럼 / 같이**
- *“Like [Noun] / As… as [Noun]”*

### 2) Core Meaning
