This is magic dust you use to vividly describe something by comparing it to something else! Saying “천사처럼 착해요” (Kind like an angel) feels much more vivid than just saying someone is kind, right? It’s an expression that makes your words more poetic and charming!

### 3) Grammar

- Attach it right after a noun. It must be attached without any spaces.
- ‘처럼’ and ‘같이’ can be swapped as they have almost perfectly identical meanings.

### 4) How it’s used

- When complimenting: “영화배우처럼 예뻐요.”
- When describing actions: “한국 사람처럼 한국말을 잘해요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**천사** (Angel),**바다** (Ocean),**눈** (Snow),**한국 사람** (Korean person)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **천사처럼** (Like an angel)
- **바다처럼** (Like the ocean)
- **눈처럼** (Like snow)
- **한국 사람처럼** (Like a Korean)
- **영화배우같이** (Like a movie star)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **바다처럼 마음이 넓어요.** (He/She has a broad mind like the ocean.)
- **한국 사람처럼 한국말을 잘해요.** (You speak Korean well like a Korean.)
- **영화배우같이 생겼어요.** (You look like a movie star.)
- **눈처럼 하얀 피부예요.** (Skin as white as snow.)
- **저처럼 해 보세요.** (Try doing it like me.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Insert the appropriate metaphor.

1. (Angel) 그녀는 _______________ 노래해요.
2. (Me) _______________ 해 보세요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 천사처럼 (or 천사같이) / 2. 저처럼 (or 저같이)

### 9) My Sentence

Who do you want to be like?

- 저는 _______________처럼 되고 싶어요.

---

## 💎 Special Unit 3. 지옥의 한국어 숫자 (Numbers: Sino vs Native)

“일, 이, 삼” vs “하나, 둘, 셋”

Before learning the patterns for quantities, prices, and time, there is a mountain you must climb. It’s ‘Numbers’, the thing that frustrates learners the most when studying Korean. There are two number systems in Korean: Sino-Korean numbers and Native Korean numbers.

It’s confusing, but if you know the core rules and how to combine them, you can conquer it much easier than you think!

**1. 한자어 숫자 (Sino-Korean Numbers)**

Mainly used for math-related information like calculating, money, phone numbers, or when saying large/long numbers.

- **영/공(0), 일(1), 이(2), 삼(3), 사(4), 오(5), 육(6), 칠(7), 팔(8), 구(9), 십(10), 백(100), 천(1,000), 만(10,000)**
- **How to make 11, 20, 99? (It’s just simple math!)**
Unlike English, you don’t need to memorize new words for 11 or 20. Just add them up!
    - 11 = 10 (십) + 1 (일) =**십일**
    - 12 = 10 (십) + 2 (이) =**십이**
    - 20 = 2 (이) x 10 (십) =**이십**
    - 99 = 9 (구) x 10 (십) + 9 (구) =**구십구**
- **When to use it?** Money (천 원, 이만 원), Phone numbers (공일공-일이삼사), Dates, and**‘Minutes/Seconds’** for time (삼십 분).

**2. 고유어 숫자 (Native Korean Numbers)**

Used when counting the number of items or stating your age. It’s mainly used for counting smaller numbers and only goes up to 99.

- **하나(1), 둘(2), 셋(3), 넷(4), 다섯(5), 여섯(6), 일곱(7), 여덟(8), 아홉(9), 열(10)**
- **Tens:** **스물(20), 서른(30), 마흔(40), 쉰(50)**
- **How to make 11, 22? (Just put them together!)**
Combine the tens word and the ones word.
    - 11 = 10 (열) + 1 (하나) =**열하나**
    - 12 = 10 (열) + 2 (둘) =**열둘**
    - 13 = 10 (열) + 3 (셋) =**열셋**
    - 14 = 10 (열) + 4 (넷) =**열넷**
    - 21 = 20 (스물) + 1 (하나) =**스물하나**
    - 22 = 20 (스물) + 2 (둘) =**스물둘**
    - 23 = 20 (스물) + 3 (셋) =**스물셋**...continue with same logic.
- **When to use it?** Number of items (사과 한 개), Age (스무 살), Number of people (세 명), and**‘Hour’** for time (한 시).
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Caution (Shape Shifting!)**
> 
> 
> The numbers 1, 2, 3, 4, and 20 change their forms when they meet counting units (개, 명, 잔, 살, etc.)!
> 
> - 하나 → **한** (사과 한 개 / 1 apple)
> - 둘 → **두** (커피 두 잔 / 2 cups of coffee)
> - 셋 → **세** (사람 세 명 / 3 people)
> - 넷 → **네** (책 네 권 / 4 books)
> - 스물 → **스무** (스무 살 / 20 years old)
> - *Note:* 11 also shifts! 열하나 + 개 = **열한 개** (11 items)
> - *Note:* 12 also shifts! 열두 + 개 = **열두 개** (12 items)


**3. 가장 많이 헷갈리는 ‘시간’ (Time): 시 vs 분**

When telling time in Korean, you need BOTH number systems!

- **시 (Hour)** = Native Korean Numbers (Because hours are small and precious, use Native!)
- **분 (Minute)** = Sino-Korean Numbers (Because minutes go up to 60, use Mathematical Sino!)
- Example: 3:30 =**세** 시**삼십** 분 / 5:15 =**다섯** 시**십오** 분

**4. Practice**

✔**Practice 1.** Use the correct number systems.

1. (You want to order 2 cups of Americano at a cafe. Use Native shape-shifting) 아메리카노 _______________ 잔 주세요.
2. (You want to say it is 12:30. Hour = Native, Minute = Sino) 지금 _______________ 시 _______________ 분이에요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 두 / 2. 열두, 삼십

---


---


---




![[sulsul_pattern_33.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 Verb ]** +**는 / ㄴ/은 / ㄹ/을** +**[ 명사 Noun ]**
- *“[Noun] that [Verb]s / [Noun] that [Verb]ed / [Noun] that will [Verb]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is a powerful magic that transforms verbs to describe nouns! It’s similar to relative pronouns in English (who, which, that) but much simpler. If you know this pattern, you can richly expand your sentences like ‘내가 좋아하는 사람’ (The person I like), ‘어제 본 영화’ (The movie I watched yesterday), and ‘내일 먹을 밥’ (The meal I will eat tomorrow).

### 3) Grammar

It attaches after the verb to indicate the tense (time).

- **Present (Doing now):** Verb Stem +**는** (e.g., 먹는 밥, 가는 곳)
- **Past (Already did):** Verb Stem +**ㄴ/은** (If no Batchim: -ㄴ, If Batchim: -은) (e.g., 본 영화, 먹은 밥)
- **Future (Will do):** Verb Stem +**ㄹ/을** (If no Batchim: -ㄹ, If Batchim: -을) (e.g., 갈 곳, 먹을 밥)

### 4) How it’s used

- When talking about an experience: “어제 본 영화 진짜 재미있어.”
- When stating your taste: “내가 제일 좋아하는 가수야.”
- When talking about plans: “내일 입을 옷을 샀어.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**좋아하는** (Like),**가는** (Go),**본** (Watched/Saw),**먹은** (Ate),**입을** (Will wear),**만날** (Will meet)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **좋아하는 가수** (The singer I like)
- **자주 가는 카페** (The cafe I often go to)
- **어제 본 영화** (The movie I watched yesterday)
- **방금 먹은 밥** (The meal I just ate)
- **내일 입을 옷** (Clothes to wear tomorrow)
- **주말에 만날 친구** (Friend to meet on the weekend)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **제가 좋아하는 가수예요.** (He/She is the singer that I like.)
- **어제 만난 친구가 미국에 가요.** (The friend I met yesterday is going to the US.)
- **내일 입을 옷을 샀어요.** (I bought clothes to wear tomorrow.)
- **자주 가는 카페가 어디예요?** (Where is the cafe that you go to often?)
- **이것은 제가 만든 요리예요.** (This is the dish that I made.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher's Tip**
>
> In textbooks, you'll often see **'이것은 (이것은 = This)'**, **'그것은 (That)'**, and **'저것은 (That over there)'** — and yes, they are grammatically spot-on! But here's the thing: in real, everyday Korean conversation, native speakers almost always shorten them. **'이것은' → '이건'** / **'그것은' → '그건'** / **'저것은' → '저건'** (or even more casually, **'이거', '그거', '저거'**). So "이것은 제가 만든 요리예요" and "이거 제가 만든 거예요" mean exactly the same thing — the second just sounds like something a real Korean friend would actually say!

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Transform the verb according to the tense to describe the noun.

1. (Present) 매일 ( 마시다 ) 커피 → _______________ 커피
2. (Past) 어제 ( 보다 ) 드라마 → _______________ 드라마
3. (Future) 주말에 ( 읽다 ) 책 → _______________ 책
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 마시는 / 2. 본 / 3. 읽을

### 9) My Sentence

What is your absolute favorite food?

- 제가 제일 좋아하는 음식은 _______________이에요/예요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_34.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사/형용사 Verb/Adjective 1 ]** +**고** +**[ 동사/형용사 Verb/Adjective 2 ]**
- *“[Verb 1] and [Verb 2] / [Verb 1] and then [Verb 2]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This acts as glue smoothly connecting sentences together. It is used to list multiple actions or states, and also to state the sequence of doing one thing first and then doing another.

### 3) Grammar

- Attach **‘-고’** after the Verb or Adjective Stem. It’s always ‘-고’ regardless of whether there is a Batchim or not.
- When using the past tense, it sounds much more natural to drop the tense marker from the first verb and**only put the past tense form on the final verb**. (e.g., 밥을 먹었고 영화를 봤어요(X) → 밥을 먹고 영화를 봤어요(O))

### 4) How it’s used

- When listing features: “이거는 싸고 맛있어요.”
- When stating actions in sequence: “밥 먹고 커피 마셔요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**먹고** (Eat and),**가고** (Go and),**보고** (Watch and),**싸고** (Cheap and),**예쁘고** (Pretty and)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **먹고** (Eat and…)
- **가고** (Go and…)
- **보고** (Watch and…)
- **만나고** (Meet and…)
- **싸고** (Cheap and…)
- **예쁘고** (Pretty and…)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **밥을 먹고 커피를 마셔요.** (I eat a meal and drink coffee.)
- **이 옷은 싸고 예뻐요.** (These clothes are cheap and pretty.)
- **손을 씻고 밥을 드세요.** (Please wash your hands and then eat.)
- **어제는 비가 오고 바람이 불었어요.** (Yesterday it rained and the wind blew.)
- **친구를 만나고 영화를 봤어요.** (I met a friend and watched a movie.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Connect the two sentences naturally.

1. (운동하다) + (샤워하다) → 운동하_______________ 샤워해요.
2. (맛있다) + (싸다) → 이 식당은 맛있_______________ 싸요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 고 / 2. 고

### 9) My Sentence

State the sequence of two things you usually do after getting off work/school.

- 집에 가서 _______________고 _______________해요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_35.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 형용사/동사 어간 Adjective/Verb Stem ]** +**아/어서**
- *“Because [Adjective / Verb], so…”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is the pattern used when explaining ‘why’ something happened. You state the reason first, and then the result follows. It connects cause and effect naturally, like “비가 와서 집에 있었어요” (Because it rained, I stayed home). It’s an absolute must-have for making excuses(?)!

### 3) Grammar

Attach **‘-아/어서’** to the Adjective/Verb Stem. (Same as the past tense rules)

- If there’s an ㅏ or ㅗ, use ‘-아서’. If not, use ‘-어서’. ‘하다’ changes to ‘해서’.
- **CAUTION 1:** You must**NEVER use the past tense marker (-았/었-)** in front of ‘-아/어서’. Even if it’s a past event, attach it to the base form! (e.g., 바빴어서 (X) →**바빠서 (O)**)
- **CAUTION 2:** You cannot use suggestions or commands like “합시다” or “하세요” in the following sentence. (For those, you must use ‘-(으)니까’.)

### 4) How it’s used

- When explaining a reason: “배가 고파서 밥을 먹었어요.”
- When apologizing for being late: “늦어서 죄송합니다.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**바빠서** (Because I’m busy),**아파서** (Because it hurts),**피곤해서** (Because I’m tired),**늦어서** (Because I’m late)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **바빠서** (Because I’m busy)
- **아파서** (Because I’m sick/it hurts)
- **피곤해서** (Because I’m tired)
- **늦어서** (Because I’m late)
- **비싸서** (Because it’s expensive)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **배가 아파서 병원에 갔어요.** (Because my stomach hurt, I went to the hospital.)
- **피곤해서 일찍 잤어요.** (Because I was tired, I slept early.)
- **날씨가 좋아서 산책을 해요.** (Because the weather is good, I take a walk.)
- **늦어서 죄송합니다.** (I’m sorry because I’m late.)
- **너무 비싸서 안 샀어요.** (It was too expensive, so I didn’t buy it.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (The Secret of Greetings)**
> 
> 
> When meeting someone for the first time, you say “만나서 반갑습니다” (Nice to meet you), right? That actually comes from this grammar: “만나게 되어서 (Reason: Because I got to meet you) 반갑습니다 (Result: I am glad)”!
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Create a reason and connect it.

1. (비가 오다) + (안 나갔어요) → 비가 _______________ 안 나갔어요.
2. (바쁘다) + (못 갔어요) → _______________ 못 갔어요. (Attach to the base form even for past events!)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 와서 / 2. 바빠서

### 9) My Sentence

Is there a reason you feel tired or in a good mood today?

- 저는 _______________아서/어서 _______________.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_36.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 형용사/동사 어간 Adjective/Verb Stem ]** +**지만**
- *“[Adjective / Verb], but…”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is a magic word that brings out the charm of a plot twist! It is used when you acknowledge something in the front, and then pull out a different story in the back saying, “But on the other hand~”. Creating this ‘A but B’ structure makes your sentences flow much more elegantly.

### 3) Grammar

This is the easiest Korean grammar. Just drop ‘다’ from the dictionary form and attach **‘-지만’**, and you’re done! It doesn’t change whether there’s a Batchim or not.

- When talking about the past, attach it right after the past tense marker. (e.g., 먹었지만, 갔지만)

### 4) How it’s used

- When covering up a flaw: “비싸지만 예뻐요.”
- When stating contrasting situations: “한국어는 어렵지만 재미있어요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**좋지만** (Good but),**싫지만** (Dislike but),**비싸지만** (Expensive but),**어렵지만** (Difficult but)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **좋지만** (It’s good, but…)
- **싫지만** (I hate it, but…)
- **있지만** (I have it, but…)
- **없지만** (I don’t have it, but…)
- **비싸지만** (It’s expensive, but…)
- **어렵지만** (It’s difficult, but…)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **한국어는 어렵지만 재미있어요.** (Korean is difficult, but it’s fun.)
- **비싸지만 샀어요.** (It was expensive, but I bought it.)
- **김치를 먹었지만 맵지 않았어요.** (I ate kimchi, but it wasn’t spicy.)
- **돈은 없지만 시간은 많아요.** (I don’t have money, but I have a lot of time.)
- **죄송하지만 안 돼요.** (I’m sorry, but it’s not allowed. - Polite refusal)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Create a contrasting sentence.

1. (덥다) + (에어컨이 없어요) → 덥_______________ 에어컨이 없어요.
2. (실례다) + (누구세요?) → 실례_______________ 누구세요?
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 지만 / 2. 지만

### 9) My Sentence

Connect the pros and cons of studying Korean with a twist!

- 한국어는 _______________지만 _______________해요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_37.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**기 전에**
- *“Before [Verb]ing”*

### 2) Core Meaning

It is used to indicate the timing ‘prior to’ doing something. It is very useful for explaining preparations or situations that need to be done in advance of an important event. It’s an essential everyday pattern, like “밥 먹기 전에 손을 씻어요” (Wash your hands before eating).

### 3) Grammar

- Attach **‘-기 전에’** right after the Verb Stem. Regardless of the Batchim, it’s very simple!
- When attaching to a Noun, drop ‘기’ and just attach**‘전에’** directly after the Noun. (e.g., 식사 전에, 회의 전에)

### 4) How it’s used

- When stating sequence: “자기 전에 씻어요.”
- When giving a heads up: “출발하기 전에 전화해 주세요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**자기 전에** (Before sleeping),**먹기 전에** (Before eating),**가기 전에** (Before going),**오기 전에** (Before coming)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **자기 전에** (Before sleeping)
- **먹기 전에** (Before eating)
- **가기 전에** (Before going)
- **오기 전에** (Before coming)
- **출발하기 전에** (Before departing)
- **수업 전에** (Before class - Noun form)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **자기 전에 스마트폰을 봐요.** (I look at my smartphone before sleeping.)
- **한국에 오기 전에 한국어를 공부했어요.** (I studied Korean before coming to Korea.)
- **밥 먹기 전에 손을 씻으세요.** (Please wash your hands before eating.)
- **출발하기 전에 전화해 주세요.** (Please call me before you depart.)
- **사기 전에 생각하세요.** (Think before buying.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Transform properly.

1. (자다) _______________ 전에 폰을 봐요.
2. (비행기를 타다) 비행기 _______________ 전에 여권을 확인하세요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 자기 / 2. 타기

### 9) My Sentence

What is the routine you always do before performing a certain action?

- 저는 _______________기 전에 _______________해요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_38.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**ㄴ/은 후에 / 고 나서**
- *“After [Verb]ing”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is the exact opposite of ‘-기 전에’ we just learned! It is used to say that you will do something else AFTER an action is completely finished. It’s an expression that clearly shows two events happening in sequential order.

### 3) Grammar

- **[은/ㄴ 후에]:** If there’s no Batchim, attach ‘-ㄴ 후에’. If there’s a Batchim, attach ‘-은 후에’. (After a Noun, just attach ‘후에’. e.g., 퇴근 후에)
- **[고 나서]:** Unconditionally attach ‘-고 나서’ after the Verb Stem. (The meaning and usage are almost identical to ‘-은 후에’!)

### 4) How it’s used

- When stating sequence of events: “밥을 먹은 후에 커피를 마셔요.”
- When making plans: “일 끝난 후에 만날까요?”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**먹은 후에** (After eating),**끝난 후에** (After finishing),**퇴근한 후에** (After getting off work),**씻고 나서** (After washing)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **먹은 후에** (After eating)
- **끝난 후에** (After finishing)
- **일한 후에** (After working)
- **퇴근한 후에** (After getting off work)
- **씻고 나서** (After washing)
- **하고 나서** (After doing)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **밥을 먹은 후에 커피를 마셔요.** (I drink coffee after eating a meal.)
- **숙제를 끝내고 나서 놀았어요.** (I played after finishing my homework.)
- **대학교를 졸업한 후에 취직했어요.** (I got a job after graduating from university.)
- **운동을 하고 나서 샤워를 해요.** (I take a shower after exercising.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Native Skill)**
> 
> 
> Instead of ‘-은 후에 (eun hu-e)’, you can use **‘-은 다음에 (eun da-eum-e)’** or **‘-은 뒤에 (eun dwi-e)’** and the meaning remains completely the same. You can comfortably choose whichever rolls off your tongue better!
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Transform properly.

1. (수업이 끝나다) 수업이 _______________ 후에 만나요.
2. (손을 씻다) 손을 _______________ 나서 먹어요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 끝난 / 2. 씻고

### 9) My Sentence

What do you frequently do after finishing something?

- 저는 _______________은/ㄴ 후에 _______________해요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_39.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 형용사/동사 어간 Adjective/Verb Stem ]** +**을/ㄹ 때**
- *“When [Adjective / Verb]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This expression pinpoints the exact timing when something happens or a specific state. It’s the perfect pattern to ask or answer what happens in certain moments, like “심심할 때 뭐 해요?” (What do you do when you’re bored?).

### 3) Grammar

- Attach it to the Adjective/Verb Stem. If there’s no Batchim, it’s **‘-ㄹ 때’**, and if there’s a Batchim, it’s**‘-을 때’**.
- After a Noun, simply attach**‘때’**! (e.g., 학생 때, 방학 때)
- When talking about a moment in the past, attach ‘-을 때’ after the past tense marker. (e.g., 어렸을 때, 한국에 갔을 때)

### 4) How it’s used

- When stating a specific situation: “비가 올 때 우산을 써요.”
- When expressing a mood: “피곤할 때 커피를 마셔요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**갈 때** (When going),**먹을 때** (When eating),**피곤할 때** (When tired),**어릴 때** (When young),**바쁠 때** (When busy)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **갈 때** (When going)
- **올 때** (When coming)
- **먹을 때** (When eating)
- **잘 때** (When sleeping)
- **피곤할 때** (When tired)
- **어릴 때** (When young)
- **바쁠 때** (When busy)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **시간이 있을 때 영화를 봐요.** (When I have time, I watch movies.)
- **어렸을 때 시골에 살았어요.** (When I was young, I lived in the countryside.)
- **스트레스 받을 때 매운 음식을 먹어요.** (When I get stressed, I eat spicy food.)
- **길을 모를 때 사람들에게 물어보세요.** (When you don’t know the way, please ask people.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Pronunciation)**
> 
> 
> ‘때 (ttae)’ has a strong tense sound. It sounds natural when you pronounce it strongly and shortly as ‘때!’. Shall we try practicing “학생 때 (hak-saeng ttae)” out loud?
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Transform properly.

1. (스트레스 받다) 스트레스 _______________ 때 매운 걸 먹어요.
2. (비행기를 타다) 비행기 _______________ 때 멀미를 해요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 받을 / 2. 탈

### 9) My Sentence

What action do you usually take when you are in a specific mood or situation?

- 저는 _______________을/ㄹ 때 _______________해요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_40.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 형용사/동사 어간 Adjective/Verb Stem ]** +**을/ㄹ 때마다**
- *“Every time (whenever) [Adjective / Verb]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is the upgraded version of ‘-을/ㄹ 때’ we just learned! It’s used when emphasizing that the same thing repeats ‘every time, without exception’ whenever a condition is met, not just once. It’s great for expressing regular habits or emotions.

### 3) Grammar

- The rules to make this form are identical to ‘-을/ㄹ 때’. You just slap on **‘마다’** and you’re done!
- Attaching ‘마다’ right after a Noun means ‘every/each’. (e.g., 주말마다, 사람마다)

### 4) How it’s used

- When talking about regular occurrences: “한국에 올 때마다 이 식당에 와요.”
- When expressing conditioned emotions: “그 노래를 들을 때마다 슬퍼요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**갈 때마다** (Whenever I go),**볼 때마다** (Whenever I see),**만날 때마다** (Whenever I meet),**비가 올 때마다** (Whenever it rains)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **갈 때마다** (Whenever I go)
- **올 때마다** (Whenever I come)
- **들을 때마다** (Whenever I hear)
- **볼 때마다** (Whenever I see)
- **만날 때마다** (Whenever I meet)
- **비가 올 때마다** (Whenever it rains)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **그 노래를 들을 때마다 눈물이 나요.** (Every time I hear that song, tears come out.)
- **한국에 올 때마다 이 식당에 와요.** (Whenever I come to Korea, I come to this restaurant.)
- **비가 올 때마다 파전이 먹고 싶어요.** (Whenever it rains, I want to eat pajeon.)
- **어머니는 저를 볼 때마다 웃어요.** (My mother smiles every time she sees me.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Transform properly.

1. (음악을 듣다) 이 음악을 _______________ 때마다 신나요.
2. (사진을 보다) 옛날 사진을 _______________ 때마다 웃음이 나요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 들을 / 2. 볼

### 9) My Sentence

Is there an emotion or recurring event you always experience whenever a specific situation arises?

- 저는 _______________을/ㄹ 때마다 _______________해요.

---

 


![[survival_mission_4.png|700]]


![[chapter_05.png|700]]

![[sulsul_pattern_41.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**아/어야 해요**
- *“Have to [Verb] / Must [Verb]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

It’s used to indicate a strong obligation or necessity that an action must be done. It’s a pattern you use when you want to say, “This is not an option, it’s a must!”.

### 3) Grammar

If the last vowel of the verb is ‘ㅏ’ or ‘ㅗ’, use **‘-아야 해요’**. For all other vowels, use**‘-어야 해요’**. ‘하다’ changes to**‘해야 해요’**. (It’s exactly the same as the past tense 아/어요 rules!)

### 4) How it’s used

- When stating your schedule: “지금 출발해야 해요.”
- When stating advice or rules: “마스크를 써야 해요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**가야 해요** (Have to go),**해야 해요** (Have to do),**공부해야 해요** (Have to study),**자야 해요** (Have to sleep)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **가야 해요.** (I have to go.)
- **해야 해요.** (I have to do it.)
- **공부해야 해요.** (I have to study.)
- **일해야 해요.** (I have to work.)
- **자야 해요.** (I have to sleep.)
- **먹어야 해요.** (I have to eat.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **내일 일찍 일어나야 해요.** (I have to wake up early tomorrow.)
- **숙제를 끝내야 해요.** (I have to finish my homework.)
- **약을 먹어야 해요.** (I have to take medicine.)
- **지금 빨리 출발해야 해요.** (I have to leave quickly now.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Native Skill)**
> 
> 
> In real conversation, people very often use ‘돼요 (from 되다)’ instead of ‘해요’ and say **‘-(아/어)야 돼요’**. Both mean “have to”, so you can mix and match them perfectly!
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Change to words indicating duty.

1. (A student’s duty) 학생은 _______________ 해요. (공부하다)
2. (For your health) 밥을 잘 _______________ 해요. (먹다)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 공부해야 / 2. 먹어야

### 9) My Sentence

What is something you absolutely ‘must do’ today?

- 저는 오늘 꼭 _______________아/어야 해요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_42.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**아/어야겠다**
- *“I should [Verb] / I guess I have to [Verb]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Use this when you see a situation and make up your mind, talking to yourself: “Ah, I guess I should do this!”. It’s not a command to someone else, but a soft resolution directed at yourself.

### 3) Grammar

Depending on the last vowel of the verb, it’s divided into **‘-아야겠다 / -어야겠다 / -해야겠다’**. When talking to yourself, use the short form ‘-아/어야겠다’, and when telling someone else about your resolution, use the polite form ‘-아/어야겠어요’.

### 4) How it’s used

- When making a resolution: “다이어트 해야겠다.”
- When a situation changes: “비 오네. 우산 가져가야겠다.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**자야겠다** (Should sleep),**가야겠다** (Should go),**먹어야겠다** (Should eat),**사야겠다** (Should buy)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **자야겠다.** (I should sleep.)
- **가야겠다.** (I should go.)
- **먹어야겠다.** (I should eat.)
- **해야겠다.** (I should do it.)
- **사야겠다.** (I should buy it.)
- **쉬어야겠다.** (I should rest.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **비가 오네요. 우산을 가져가야겠어요.** (It’s raining. I should take an umbrella.)
- **너무 피곤해요. 일찍 자야겠어요.** (I’m too tired. I should go to bed early.)
- **살이 쪘어요. 운동을 시작해야겠다.** (I gained weight. I guess I should start exercising.)
- **배고프다. 빨리 밥 먹어야겠다.** (I’m hungry. I should eat quickly.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Create a resolution talking to yourself.

1. (When you’re tired) 오늘은 집에서 푹 _______________. (쉬다)
2. (A movie a friend said was fun) 그 영화 재미있겠다. 나도 _______________. (보다)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 쉬어야겠다 / 2. 봐야겠다

### 9) My Sentence

What is something you recently resolved, “I should do this”?

- 내일부터 꼭 _______________아/어야겠다.

## 

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_43.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 형용사/동사 어간 Adjective/Verb Stem ]** +**으면/면**
- *“If [Adjective/Verb] / When [Adjective/Verb]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

It is used when stating a condition or an assumption. Just like ‘If’ or ‘When’ in English, it carries the meanings of both “If I do~” or “When I do~”.

### 3) Grammar

If the stem has a Batchim, attach **‘-으면’**. If there’s no Batchim or it ends with ‘ㄹ’, attach**‘-면’**. You can use it freely with both adjectives and verbs.

### 4) How it’s used

- When making an assumption: “내일 비가 오면 집에 있을 거예요.”
- When giving advice: “이 약을 먹으면 괜찮아질 거예요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**가면** (If you go),**오면** (If you come),**하면** (If you do),**먹으면** (If you eat),**비싸면** (If it’s expensive),**좋으면** (If it’s good)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **가면** (If you go)
- **오면** (If it comes)
- **하면** (If you do it)
- **먹으면** (If you eat)
- **비싸면** (If it’s expensive)
- **좋으면** (If it’s good)
- **아프면** (If it hurts)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **비가 오면 집에 있을 거예요.** (If it rains, I will stay at home.)
- **시간이 있으면 같이 커피 마셔요.** (If you have time, let’s drink coffee together.)
- **모르는 게 있으면 물어보세요.** (If you have something you don’t know, please ask.)
- **날씨가 좋으면 한강에 가고 싶어요.** (If the weather is good, I want to go to the Han River.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Native Skill)**
> 
> 
> If you use words like **“만약 (man-yak)”** or **“만일 (ma-nil)”** at the very front of the sentence, you can emphasize the assumption even more. Like “만약 비가 오면~”!
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Set a condition.

1. (Weather condition) 내일 날씨가 _______________ 바다에 가요. (좋다)
2. (Money condition) 이거 너무 _______________ 사지 마세요. (비싸다)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 좋으면 / 2. 비싸면

### 9) My Sentence

Make an assumption about a situation that could happen.

- 만약에 내일 비가 _______________면 _______________할 거예요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_44.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**면 안 돼요 / 아/어도 돼요?**
- *“You shouldn’t [Verb] / May I [Verb]?”*

### 2) Core Meaning

These are a perfect pair of expressions used when strictly prohibiting someone from doing something (“You shouldn’t do this”), or conversely, when politely asking for permission saying, “Is it okay if I do this?”.

### 3) Grammar

- **When prohibiting:** Depending on the Batchim, use**‘-으면 안 돼요 / -면 안 돼요’**.
- **When asking for permission:** Depending on the vowel, attach**‘-아도 돼요? / -어도 돼요? / -해도 돼요?’**. (Identical to past tense rules)

### 4) How it’s used

- When explaining rules: “여기서 담배 피우면 안 돼요.”
- When asking for permission at a store/restaurant: “이거 입어봐도 돼요?”, “여기 앉아도 돼요?”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**하면 안 돼요** (Shouldn’t do),**먹으면 안 돼요** (Shouldn’t eat),**가도 돼요?** (May I go?),**앉아도 돼요?** (May I sit?)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **가면 안 돼요.** (You shouldn’t go.)
- **먹으면 안 돼요.** (You shouldn’t eat it.)
- **가도 돼요?** (May I go?)
- **앉아도 돼요?** (May I sit?)
- **먹어도 돼요?** (May I eat it?)
- **해도 돼요?** (May I do it?)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **여기서 사진을 찍으면 안 돼요.** (You shouldn’t take pictures here.)
- **극장에서 전화하면 안 돼요.** (You shouldn’t make phone calls in the theater.)
- **여기 앉아도 돼요?** (May I sit here?)
- **내일 일찍 가도 돼요?** (May I go early tomorrow?)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Native Skill)**
> 
> 
> When asked “해도 돼요?”, you could reply positively with “네, 해도 돼요”, but native speakers very often simply reply with **“네, 괜찮아요 (gwaen-cha-na-yo)”**!
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Prohibit or ask for permission.

1. (When touching a painting at an art museum) 그림을 _______________면 안 돼요. (만지다)
2. (When you need to use a friend’s computer) 컴퓨터 좀 _______________도 돼요? (쓰다)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 만지 / 2. 써

### 9) My Sentence

Write down an action you know is absolutely prohibited, or a situation where you need to ask for permission.

- 여기서 _______________면 안 돼요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_45.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**지 마세요 / 지 말고**
- *“Please don’t [Verb] / Don’t [Verb] and (do something else)”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Use this when earnestly asking or commanding someone NOT to do a certain action. In particular, **‘-지 말고’** is an incredibly useful pattern when suggesting a better alternative, saying “Don’t do that, do this instead.”

### 3) Grammar

It’s super simple! Regardless of whether there’s a Batchim or not, just attach**‘-지 마세요’** or**‘-지 말고’** directly to the Verb Stem!

### 4) How it’s used

- When comforting or holding someone back: “걱정하지 마세요.”, “울지 마세요.”
- When suggesting a better way: “버스 타지 말고 택시 타요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**가지 마세요** (Don’t go),**하지 마세요** (Don’t do),**걱정하지 마세요** (Don’t worry),**기다리지 말고** (Don’t wait and…)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **가지 마세요.** (Please don’t go.)
- **하지 마세요.** (Please don’t do it.)
- **걱정하지 마세요.** (Please don’t worry.)
- **울지 마세요.** (Please don’t cry.)
- **가지 말고** (Don’t go and…)
- **먹지 말고** (Don’t eat and…)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **너무 늦게 자지 마세요.** (Please don’t sleep too late.)
- **버스 타지 말고 지하철을 타세요.** (Don’t take the bus, take the subway instead.)
- **커피 마시지 말고 차를 마셔요.** (Don’t drink coffee, drink tea instead.)
- **혼자 고민하지 말고 저한테 이야기하세요.** (Don’t worry alone and talk to me.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Native Skill)**
> 
> 
> When speaking casually among friends, drop ‘요’ and say **“~지 마! (ji ma!)”**. You’ve probably heard lines like “울지 마 (ul-ji ma)” or “가지 마 (ga-ji ma)” in K-dramas a lot, right?
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Change to an expression of prohibition/stopping.

1. (When touching a dangerous object) 이거 _______________ 마세요. (만지다)
2. (When going to see a movie) 집에서 _______________ 말고 극장에 가요. (보다)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 만지지 / 2. 보지

### 9) My Sentence

Suggest a better action to someone.

- _______________지 말고 _______________으세요.

* *

![[sulsul_pattern_46.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**는 게 어때요? / 는 게 좋겠어요**
- *“How about [Verb]ing? / It would be better to [Verb]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Instead of commanding “~해라!” (Do this!), this is used to give advice or make a suggestion softly and politely. It’s a very sophisticated, adult way of speaking that considers the other person’s feelings while expressing your opinion.

### 3) Grammar

- Attach ‘-는 게 어때요?’ to the Verb Stem regardless of the Batchim.
- However, for verbs ending with a ‘ㄹ’ Batchim, the ‘ㄹ’ drops out! (e.g., 만들다 → **만드는 게 어때요?**)

### 4) How it’s used

- When advising treatment or rest: “조금 쉬는 게 어때요?”
- When suggesting an alternative: “택시를 타는 게 좋겠어요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**쉬는 게** (Resting),**가는 게** (Going),**사는 게** (Buying),**타는 게** (Riding)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **쉬는 게 어때요?** (How about resting?)
- **가는 게 어때요?** (How about going?)
- **사는 게 좋겠어요.** (It would be better to buy it.)
- **타는 게 좋겠어요.** (It would be better to take a ride.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **병원에 가는 게 어때요?** (How about going to the hospital?)
- **이 옷을 사는 게 좋겠어요.** (It would be better to buy these clothes.)
- **택시를 타는 게 좋겠어요.** (It would be better to take a taxi.)
- **선생님께 물어보는 게 어때요?** (How about asking the teacher?)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Native Skill)**
> 
> 
> With close friends, you can drop ‘요’ and just ask lightly, “병원에 가는 게 어때?”. It gives a soft feeling of respecting the other person’s wishes.
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Give advice suitable for the situation.

1. (When advising a sick-looking friend to go to the hospital) 병원에 _______________?
2. (When suggesting taking a taxi because you might be late) 택시를 _______________.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 가는 게 어때요 / 2. 타는 게 좋겠어요

### 9) My Sentence

Imagine a friend is agonizing over a problem. Give them gentle advice.

- 그러면 _______________는 게 어때요?

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_47.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**려고 / 기 위해서**
- *“In order to [Verb]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Used to express the ‘purpose’ or ‘intention’ of doing a certain action. “-기 위해서” indicates a slightly more formal and stronger purpose, while “-려고” is used much more often in daily life and conversation.

### 3) Grammar

- **려고:** If there is NO Batchim or a ‘ㄹ’ Batchim, use ‘-려고’. If there IS a Batchim, use ‘-으려고’.
- **기 위해서:** Just attach it directly to the dictionary Verb Stem.

### 4) How it’s used

- When revealing the reason for an action: “한국어를 배우려고 한국에 왔어요.”
- When explaining a goal: “시험에 합격하기 위해서 공부해요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**배우려고** (To learn),**빼려고** (To lose weight),**만나려고** (To meet),**합격하기 위해서** (To pass)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **배우려고** (In order to learn)
- **빼려고** (In order to lose)
- **만나려고** (In order to meet)
- **합격하기 위해서** (In order to pass)
- **유지하기 위해서** (In order to maintain)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **한국어를 배우려고 한국에 왔어요.** (I came to Korea in order to learn Korean.)
- **살을 빼려고 운동을 해요.** (I exercise in order to lose weight.)
- **건강을 유지하기 위해서 비타민을 먹어요.** (I take vitamins to maintain my health.)
- **시험에 합격하기 위해서 열심히 공부했어요.** (I studied hard to pass the exam.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Connect with words showing purpose.

1. (I want to make Korean friends, so I do language exchange) 한국 친구를 _______________ 언어 교환을 해요.
2. (I want to get a job, so I prepare for an interview) 취직하_______________ 면접을 준비해요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 만들려고 (or 만들기 위해서) / 2. 기 위해서 (or 취직하려고)

### 9) My Sentence

What is your biggest purpose for studying Korean?

- 저는 _______________으려고/려고 한국어를 공부해요.

---

## 💎 Special Unit 4. 사계절과 날씨 (Four Seasons & Weather)

“오늘 날씨가 정말 맑아요!” (The weather is really clear today!)

Korea has very distinct four seasons, so the charm of each season is different, and the clothes you wear change drastically. Therefore, talking about the weather like “비가 오나 봐요” (I guess it’s raining) is also the best way to start small talk in Korea.

**1. 사계절 (The Four Seasons)**

- **봄 (Spring):** 따뜻하다(warm). 벚꽃(cherry blossoms)이 피는 아름다운 계절이에요. 가끔 황사(yellow dust)가 오기도 해요.
- **여름 (Summer):** 덥다(hot), 습하다(humid). 한국의 여름은 장마(monsoon)가 있어서 아주 덥고 후덥지근해요.
- **가을 (Autumn/Fall):** 시원하다(cool), 쌀쌀하다(chilly). 단풍(autumn leaves)이 예쁘고 일교차(temperature difference)가 커서 감기에 조심해야 해요.
- **겨울 (Winter):** 춥다(cold). 눈이 오고(snows) 얼음이 얼어요. 두꺼운 패딩(padded jacket)이 필수랍니다!

**2. 일상적인 날씨 표현 (Daily Weather Expressions)**

Shall we say how the weather is today?

- **날씨가 좋아요 / 나빠요.** (The weather is good / bad.)
- **비가 와요 / 눈이 와요.** (It’s raining / snowing.)
- **바람이 불어요.** (The wind is blowing.)
- **흐려요 / 맑아요.** (It’s cloudy / clear.)
- **미세먼지 나쁨.** (Bad fine dust) - This is a word you must check on your weather app in Spring or Autumn! Because you’ll need to wear a mask.

**3. Practice**

✔**Practice 1.** Describe today’s weather.

1. (Korea’s summer has a lot of rain and is very…) 한국의 여름은 비가 많이 오고 아주 _______________.
2. (In winter, the weather is cold and it snows a lot) 겨울에는 날씨가 춥고 _______________이/가 많이 와요.
3. (When you want to ask a Korean friend about the weather) 오늘 날씨가 _______________?
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 습해요 (or 더워요) / 2. 눈 / 3. 어때요

---


---


---


 

![[sulsul_pattern_48.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 형용사/동사 어간 Adjective/Verb Stem ]** +**다고 하다 (대요)**
- *“(Someone) said that [Adjective/Verb]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Used to convey what someone else said or information like news to a third party. In everyday conversation, the long sentence ‘-다고 해요’ is usually shortened to **‘-대요’** so you can speak more comfortably!

### 3) Grammar

- **Adjective:** The base form + ‘-다고 해요(대요)’. (e.g., 바쁘다 →**바쁘대요**)
- **Verb:** If no Batchim, ‘-ㄴ다고 해요(ㄴ대요)’. If Batchim, ‘-는다고 해요(는대요)’. (e.g., 가다 →**간대요** / 먹다 →**먹는대요**)
- **Noun:** ‘-(이)라고 해요(래요)’. (e.g., 학생 →**학생이래요**)

### 4) How it’s used

- When passing on a rumor: “그 식당 음식이 정말 맛있대요.”
- When delivering the weather forecast or news: “내일 비가 온대요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**바쁘대요** (Said they are busy),**온대요** (Said it’s coming/raining),**맛있대요** (Said it’s delicious)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **바쁘대요.** (Said they are busy.)
- **온대요.** (Said it’s coming/raining.)
- **간대요.** (Said they are going.)
- **맛있대요.** (Said it’s delicious.)
- **학생이래요.** (Said they are a student.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **지민 씨가 요즘 아주 바쁘대요.** (Jimin said she is very busy these days.)
- **내일 비가 온대요.** (They say it will rain tomorrow.)
- **민호 씨가 매일 운동한대요.** (Minho said he exercises every day.)
- **그 식당 음식이 정말 맛있대요.** (I heard the food at that restaurant is really delicious.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip**
> 
> 
> Words containing ‘있다/없다’ like ‘맛있다/재미있다’ do NOT use the verb form ‘-는대요’. Instead, attach ‘-대요’ like an adjective to make: **‘맛있대요’, ‘재미있대요’**!
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Convey a story you heard.

1. (A friend said they eat spicy food well) 친구가 매운 것을 잘 _______________.
2. (The weather forecast said it will snow tomorrow) 내일 눈이 _______________.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 먹는대요 (or 먹는다고 해요) / 2. 온대요 (or 온다고 해요)

### 9) My Sentence

Is there any interesting news you heard recently from a friend or the news?

- _______________대요/ㄴ대요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_49.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**냐고 하다 (냬요)**
- *“(Someone) asked if/what/where…”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Used to convey a question someone asked you to another person. It’s like saying, “That person asked when you’re coming.” In conversation, it is often shortened to **‘-냬요’**.

### 3) Grammar

- Attach**‘-냐고 하다(냬요)’** after the Verb or Adjective Stem regardless of the Batchim. (e.g., 가다 →**가냐고 해요**)
- You can easily attach it to past (-았/었냐고 하다) and future (-을 거냐고 하다) forms too.
- For Nouns, use ‘-(이)냐고 하다’.

### 4) How it’s used

- When conveying someone’s question: “선생님이 숙제 다 했냐고 하셨어요.”
- When passing on someone asking how you are: “엄마가 밥 먹었냬요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**했냐고** (Asked if did),**거냐고** (Asked if will),**먹었냬요** (Asked if ate)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **가냐고 해요.** (Asked if going.)
- **먹었냐고 해요.** (Asked if ate.)
- **언제냐고 해요.** (Asked when it is.)
- **뭐냐고 해요.** (Asked what it is.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **선생님이 숙제를 다 했냐고 하셨어요.** (The teacher asked if I finished the homework.)
- **친구가 언제 만날 거냐고 물어봤어요.** (My friend asked when we are going to meet.)
- **엄마가 밥 먹었냬요.** (Mom asked if we ate.)
- **지훈 씨가 이거 누구 거냐고 물어봤어요.** (Jihun asked whose this is.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip**
> 
> 
> In real conversation, **‘-냐고 물어보다 (mu-reo-bo-da)’** is used much more often than ‘-냐고 하다’. If you say “밥 먹었냐고 물어봤어요” (They asked if I ate), it’s absolutely perfect!
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Convey the question you were asked.

1. (A friend asked you where you are going) 친구가 어디 _______________ 물어봤어요.
2. (Mom asked you if you are tired) 엄마가 _______________ 물어봤어요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 가냐고 / 2. 피곤하냐고

### 9) My Sentence

Convey a question someone recently asked you to another person.

- _______________이/가 저한테 _______________냐고 물어봤어요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_50.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**자고 하다 (재요)**
- *“(Someone) suggested let’s [Verb]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Used to convey someone’s suggestion of “Let’s do ~ together!” to another person. This is the pattern used when saying, “That person suggested we eat together.” In conversation, just say it shortly as **‘-재요’**.

### 3) Grammar

Since this is purely suggesting an action, it can**ONLY be used with Verbs**. Regardless of whether the stem has a Batchim or not, just attach**‘-자고 하다(재요)’** and you’re done!

### 4) How it’s used

- When conveying a date or plan suggestion: “친구가 주말에 영화 보자고 했어요.”
- When conveying someone’s urging: “빨리 출발하자고 하셨어요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**보자고** (Suggested to watch),**먹재요** (Suggested to eat),**하자고** (Suggested to do)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **가자고 해요.** (Suggested to go.)
- **먹자고 해요.** (Suggested to eat.)
- **보자고 해요.** (Suggested to watch.)
- **하재요.** (Suggested to do.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **친구가 주말에 같이 영화 보자고 했어요.** (My friend suggested we watch a movie together on the weekend.)
- **수미 씨가 같이 점심 먹재요.** (Sumi suggested let’s eat lunch together.)
- **내일 도서관에서 공부하자고 해요.** (They suggested let’s study at the library tomorrow.)
- **빨리 출발하자고 하셨어요.** (They suggested let’s leave quickly.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Convey the suggestion you received.

1. (A friend suggested hanging out together this evening) 친구가 오늘 저녁에 _______________.
2. (A coworker suggested grabbing a cup of coffee) 동료가 커피 _______________.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 놀자고 했어요 (or 놀재요) / 2. 한잔하자고 했어요 (or 한잔하재요)

### 9) My Sentence

Was there a fun activity someone suggested you do together?

- 친구가 같이 _______________자고 했어요.

---

 
![[sulsul_pattern_51.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**라고 하다 (래요)**
- *“(Someone) told me to [Verb]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Used to convey when someone ordered or instructed you, “Do this!” or “Please do this”. In conversation, it is shortened to **‘-래요’**.

### 3) Grammar

This is also someone ordering an action, so it is ONLY used with verbs.

- **If no Batchim:** ‘-라고 하다(래요)’ (e.g., 가다 → 가라고 해요)
- **If Batchim:** ‘-으라고 하다(으래요)’ (e.g., 먹다 → 먹으라고 해요)
- **Caution:** When conveying a request for a favor like “도와주세요” (Please help me), the form changes slightly to ‘-달라고 하다(대요)’.

### 4) How it’s used

- When conveying instructions from a doctor or parents: “엄마가 일찍 자래요.”
- When conveying warnings or rules: “조용히 하라고 했어요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**먹으라고** (Told to eat),**자래요** (Told to sleep),**하라고** (Told to do)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **가라고 해요.** (Told me to go.)
- **먹으라고 해요.** (Told me to eat.)
- **자래요.** (Told me to sleep.)
- **하래요.** (Told me to do it.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **의사 선생님이 약을 먹으라고 하셨어요.** (The doctor told me to take medicine.)
- **엄마가 일찍 자래요.** (Mom told me to go to sleep early.)
- **조용히 하라고 했어요.** (I told them to be quiet. / They told me to be quiet.)
- **매일 운동하라고 해요.** (They tell me to exercise every day.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip**
> 
> 
> When conveying a negative command like “~하지 마세요” (Don’t do ~), use **‘-지 말라고 하다(말래요)’**. (e.g., 엄마가 늦게 자지 말래요 = Mom told me not to sleep late.)
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Convey an instruction or command.

1. (The teacher instructed us to do homework every day) 선생님이 숙제를 매일 _______________.
2. (Parents told you not to go out at night) 부모님이 밤에 _______________.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 하라고 하셨어요 (or 하래요) / 2. 나가지 말라고 하셨어요 (or 나가지 말래요)

### 9) My Sentence

What was the nagging phrase your parents said to you the most when you were young?

- 부모님이 저에게 항상 _______________(으)라고 하셨어요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_52.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 형용사/동사 어간 Adjective/Verb Stem ]** +**ㄴ/은/는 것 같아요**
- *“I think [Adjective/Verb] / It seems like [Adjective/Verb]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is the ultimate soft expression that Koreans use every single day, practically living on their lips! Rather than stating your thought or opinion with 100% certainty, you use it to speak softly and humbly, saying “I think it’s ~” or “It seems like ~”.

### 3) Grammar

The rules are exactly the same as making noun modifiers (Pattern 33)!
Exception: Adjectives ending in '있다/없다' (e.g., 맛있다, 재미있다) act like verbs and take '-는'. (e.g., 맛있는 것 같아요)

- **Adjective:** If no Batchim ‘-ㄴ 것 같아요’, if Batchim ‘-은 것 같아요’ (e.g., 비싼 것 같아요 / 좋은 것 같아요)
- **Verb:** Present tense is always ‘-는 것 같아요’, past tense is ‘-ㄴ/은 것 같아요’ (e.g., 가는 것 같아요 / 간 것 같아요)
- **Noun:** ‘-인 것 같아요’

### 4) How it’s used

- When stating your opinion cautiously: “이 식당 맛있는 것 같아요.”
- When guessing a situation: “비가 올 것 같아요.” (Future tense)
- When softly mentioning your mistake: “제가 실수한 것 같아요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**맛있는 것 같아요** (Think it’s delicious),**비가 올 것 같아요** (Seems like it will rain),**실수한 것 같아요** (Think I made a mistake)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **좋은 것 같아요.** (I think it’s good.)
- **비싼 것 같아요.** (I think it’s expensive.)
- **가는 것 같아요.** (I think they are going.)
- **간 것 같아요.** (I think they went.)
- **올 것 같아요.** (I think it will come.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **이 식당 음식이 맛있는 것 같아요.** (I think the food at this restaurant is delicious.)
- **비가 올 것 같아요.** (It looks like it’s going to rain.)
- **제가 실수한 것 같아요.** (I think I made a mistake.)
- **두 사람은 친한 것 같아요.** (It seems like those two are close.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Native Skill)**
> 
> 
> Koreans frequently use “-은/는 것 같아요” to speak politely and softly, even about facts they know for sure. Saying “이거 재미있는 것 같아요” (I think this is fun) sounds much more considerate than just asserting “이거 재미있어요” (This is fun).
> 
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip (Exception Rule)**
> 
> 
> According to standard Korean grammar rules, adjectives that end with **있다/없다** (like 맛있다, 재미있다, 맛없다) are exceptions. Even though they are adjectives, they are conjugated like verbs and take **-는** instead of -은/ㄴ.
> - 재미있다 → 재미있는 것 같아요 (O) / 재미있은 것 같아요 (X)
> - 맛있다 → 맛있는 것 같아요 (O) / 맛있은 것 같아요 (X)
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Change to a soft opinion.

1. 이 영화는 재미있어요 → 이 영화는 _______________.
2. 저 사람은 한국 사람이에요 → 저 사람은 _______________.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 재미있는 것 같아요 / 2. 한국 사람인 것 같아요

### 9) My Sentence

How is your Korean study going lately? Say your opinion softly.

- 한국어는 _______________은/는 것 같아요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_53.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 형용사/동사 어간 Adjective/Verb Stem ]** +**나 봐요**
- *“It looks like / I guess [Adjective/Verb] (based on what I see)”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Used when you see a situation or clear evidence with your own eyes and guess, “Ah, so I guess that’s why!”. It is used when judging based on objective evidence, rather than a vague feeling like “-은/는 것 같아요”.

### 3) Grammar

- For Verbs, 있다/없다, and after the past tense, always use **‘-나 봐요’**.
- For Adjectives, it is grammatically correct to use ‘-(으)ㄴ가 봐요’, but in modern conversation,**‘-나 봐요’** is very commonly used even after adjectives! (e.g., 바쁘나 봐요 / 바쁜가 봐요 are both perfect!).
- For Nouns, use**‘-인가 봐요’**.

### 4) How it’s used

- When inferring a situation after seeing a clue: “우산 쓰고 가네. 비가 오나 봐요.”
- When guessing someone’s state: “연락이 없네. 바쁘나 봐요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**오나 봐요** (Looks like it’s coming),**없나 봐요** (Guess nobody is here),**바쁘나 봐요** (Guess they are busy),**맛집인가 봐요** (Looks like a famous restaurant)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **오나 봐요.** (Looks like it’s coming.)
- **없나 봐요.** (Looks like it’s not here.)
- **바쁘나 봐요.** (Looks like they are busy.)
- **아픈가 봐요.** (Looks like they are sick.)
- **학생인가 봐요.** (Looks like they are a student.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **사람들이 우산을 쓰고 있어요. 비가 오나 봐요.** (People are holding umbrellas. I guess it’s raining.)
- **불이 꺼져 있어요. 아무도 없나 봐요.** (The lights are off. It looks like nobody is here.)
- **민수 씨가 안 왔어요. 많이 바쁜가 봐요.** (Minsu didn’t come. I guess he is very busy.)
- **식당에 줄이 길어요. 맛집인가 봐요.** (The line at the restaurant is long. It looks like a famous restaurant.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip**
> 
> 
> Using the word **“아마 (a-ma, maybe/probably)”** at the front of the sentence brings out the flavor of the assumption even more!
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Make a guess based on the clue.

1. (A baby is eating bread hurriedly. They must be hungry) → 배가 _______________.
2. (That person speaks Korean very well. They must be Korean) → 한국 _______________.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 고픈가 봐요 (or 고프나 봐요) / 2. 사람인가 봐요

### 9) My Sentence

Look at what people outside are wearing and guess what the weather is like today.

- 사람들이 _______________ 입었어요. 오늘 날씨가 _______________나 봐요.


---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_54.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 형용사/동사 어간 Adjective/Verb Stem ]** +**을/ㄹ지도 몰라요**
- *“Might [Adjective/Verb] / May [Adjective/Verb]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

It indicates a very weak assumption, like “Maybe it will happen, or maybe not… but you never know, right?”. There is absolutely no certainty, but it’s used to prepare for when you can’t rule out the possibility.

### 3) Grammar

If there’s no Batchim or a ‘ㄹ’ Batchim, attach **‘-ㄹ지도 몰라요’**. If there is a Batchim, attach**‘-을지도 몰라요’**. (Exactly the same as making the future tense!) Used exactly the same for both adjectives and verbs.

### 4) How it’s used

- When preparing in advance: “오후에 비가 올지도 몰라요.” (So pack an umbrella)
- When variables might arise: “차가 막혀서 늦을지도 몰라요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**늦을지도 몰라요** (Might be late),**올지도 몰라요** (Might come/rain),**클지도 몰라요** (Might be big)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **늦을지도 몰라요.** (Might be late.)
- **올지도 몰라요.** (Might come/rain.)
- **클지도 몰라요.** (Might be big.)
- **비쌀지도 몰라요.** (Might be expensive.)
- **아플지도 몰라요.** (Might hurt/be sick.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **차가 막혀서 조금 늦을지도 몰라요.** (I might be a little late because of traffic.)
- **오후에 비가 올지도 몰라요.** (It might rain in the afternoon.)
- **그 옷이 너무 클지도 몰라요.** (Those clothes might be too big.)
- **지훈 씨가 사실을 알고 있을지도 몰라요.** (Jihun might know the truth.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip**
> 
> 
> It goes perfectly with **“혹시 (hok-si, by any chance)”**. It’s often used when taking care of someone and warning them, like “혹시 비가 올지도 몰라요 우산 챙기세요!” (By any chance it might rain, so take an umbrella!).
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Guess something that might happen.

1. (The road is very congested. We might be late for the appointment) 길이 많이 막히네요. 약속 시간에 _______________ 몰라요. (늦다)
2. (The weather is cloudy. It might snow in the evening) 날씨가 흐려요. 저녁에 눈이 _______________ 몰라요. (오다)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 늦을지도 / 2. 올지도

### 9) My Sentence

Is there any uncertain event in your schedule for tomorrow that might change?

- 내일은 혹시 _______________을/ㄹ지도 몰라요.

---

## 💎 Special Unit 5. 한국의 진짜 인사말 (Korea's Real Greetings)

"밥 먹었어요?", "언제 밥 한번 먹자!"

You already know "안녕하세요" (Hello) and "감사합니다" (Thank you), right? However, Koreans very often use greetings disguised as asking about food or making plans. Knowing these real greetings will make you sound like a total native!

**1. 밥 먹었어요? (Have you eaten?)**

Asking if you've eaten in Korea is not simply checking your meal status; it's an expression of affection and care meaning, "How are you doing these days? I care about you." You don't need to answer with the actual menu! To older people, ask politely, "식사하셨어요?".

*   **네, 먹었어요.** (Yes, I ate.) - The most standard and safe answer.
*   **아직 안 먹었어요.** (I haven't eaten yet.)

**2. 다음에 밥 한번 같이 먹어요! (Let's grab a meal sometime!)**

When parting, Koreans frequently say, "다음에 밥 먹자" (Let's eat later) or "술 한 잔 하자" (Let's grab a drink). This doesn't mean you should open your calendar and book it right now; it's a very warm and social goodbye meaning, "I hope our connection doesn't end here and continues. See you later."

**3. 수고하세요 & 조심히 가세요 (Acknowledging Hard Work & Wishing Safety)**

*   **수고하세요! (Keep up the good work!):** A warm greeting given to an employee who is still working as you leave a convenience store or restaurant.
*   **조심히 가세요. (Get home safely.):** A very common and warm goodbye wishing safety for the person heading home. It sounds much more affectionate than "안녕히 가세요".

**4. Practice**

✔**Practice 1.** Choose the real greeting that fits the situation.

1. (After you finish your work and greet to collegue still working at the office) _______________!
2. (Wrapping up warmly when saying goodbye to a friend you haven't seen in a while) 다음에 밥 한번 같이 _______________.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 수고하세요 / 2. 먹어요 (or 먹자)

---


---


---



![[sulsul_pattern_55.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 형용사/동사 어간 Adjective/Verb Stem ]** +**을/ㄹ 텐데**
- *“It should be / I would expect / I’m worried that…”*

### 2) Core Meaning

It indicates a strong assumption or certainty, acting as the background (reason) for the words that follow. It’s usually used when worrying about the other person (“피곤할 텐데 쉬세요”), or expressing your regret/sadness about a situation (“좋았을 텐데 아쉽네요”).

### 3) Grammar

If there’s no Batchim, attach **‘-ㄹ 텐데’**. If there’s a Batchim, attach**‘-을 텐데’**. It can be used in the middle of a sentence, or sometimes at the end of a conversation as “-텐데요”, trailing off to leave a lingering feeling.

### 4) How it’s used

- When worrying about and caring for the other person: “피곤할 텐데 일찍 주무세요.”
- When expressing regret: “예약해야 할 텐데요…”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**막힐 텐데** (Will probably be blocked/traffic),**피곤하실 텐데** (Must be tired),**올 텐데** (Will probably come/rain)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **막힐 텐데** (Will probably have traffic)
- **피곤할 텐데** (Must be tired)
- **비쌀 텐데** (Must be expensive)
- **올 텐데** (Will probably come)
- **좋을 텐데** (It would be good)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **지금 출발하면 차가 많이 막힐 텐데, 괜찮아요?** (There will probably be a lot of traffic if you leave now, is it okay?)
- **피곤하실 텐데 일찍 쉬세요.** (You must be tired, so please rest early.)
- **미리 예약해야 할 텐데요. (You probably need to make a reservation in advance - I’m worried you didn’t).**
- **비가 올 텐데 우산을 가져가세요.** (It will probably rain, so take an umbrella.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip**
> 
> 
> This pattern is a very warm expression that shows a Korean’s ‘걱정 (care)’. It’s full of native nuance, caring for the other person in case they are having a hard time.
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Expect a situation while worrying about the other person.

1. (A friend worked all night) 많이 _______________ 텐데 오늘은 푹 쉬세요. (피곤하다)
2. (The weather is very cold) 밖이 _______________ 텐데 따뜻하게 입고 가세요. (춥다)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 피곤할 / 2. 추울

### 9) My Sentence

Say something warmly considerate to a Korean friend predicting their situation.

- 오늘 많이 _______________을/ㄹ 텐데 _______________으세요/아라.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_56.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**기로 했어요**
- *“Decided to [Verb] / Promised to [Verb]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Used when you’ve promised to do something with someone else, or when you’ve made a firm resolution on your own. It feels like declaring a firm decision to the world: “I’m going to do it this way from now on!”.

### 3) Grammar

Just attach **‘-기로 했어요’** right after the Verb Stem, whether there’s a Batchim or not. Very simple, right? Since you’ve already ‘completed’ the resolution or promise, you always use the past tense**‘-했어요’**.

### 4) How it’s used

- When stating your resolution: “담배를 끊기로 했어요.”
- When conveying a promise with someone else: “주말에 등산하기로 했어요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**끊기로 했어요** (Decided to quit),**등산하기로 했어요** (Promised to go hiking),**외식하기로 했어요** (Decided to eat out),**걷기로 했어요** (Decided to walk)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **끊기로 했어요.** (Decided to quit.)
- **만나기로 했어요.** (Promised to meet.)
- **외식하기로 했어요.** (Decided to eat out.)
- **걷기로 했어요.** (Decided to walk.)
- **가기로 했어요.** (Decided to go.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **내년부터 담배를 끊기로 했어요.** (I decided to quit smoking from next year.)
- **친구하고 주말에 등산하기로 했어요.** (I promised to go hiking with my friend on the weekend.)
- **오늘 저녁은 외식하기로 했어요.** (We decided to eat out for dinner today.)
- **매일 30분씩 걷기로 했어요.** (I decided to walk for 30 minutes every day.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip**
> 
> 
> Unlike a casual greeting like “우리 언제 밥 한번 먹어요~” (Let’s grab a meal sometime), “금요일에 밥 먹기로 했어요” (We promised to eat on Friday) means a real ‘promise’ with a clear date and time.
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** State your resolution and promise.

1. (Diet resolution) 내일부터 매일 _______________로 했어요. (운동하다)
2. (Promise with a friend) 이번 주말에 친구랑 같이 부산에 _______________로 했어요. (가다)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 운동하기 / 2. 가기

### 9) My Sentence

What is a firm resolution you’ve made to definitely put into practice this year?

- 저는 올해 _______________기로 했어요.

---


![[sulsul_pattern_57.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**게 되다**
- *“Ended up [Verb]ing / Came to [Verb]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Used when a result or situation is reached not by your own will or original plan, but by the surrounding environment, external factors, or destiny(?). It has a somewhat passive and natural feeling, like “It just ended up flowing that way~”.

### 3) Grammar

Attach **‘-게 되다’** unchangingly after the Verb Stem. Usually, since you are talking about the result of a change that has already happened, the past tense**‘-게 되었어요’** or its shortened form**‘-게 됐어요’** is used a lot.

### 4) How it’s used

- When explaining a changed situation: “다음 달에 출장을 가게 됐어요.”
- When stating your ability has changed: “매운 음식을 잘 먹게 됐어요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**가게 됐어요** (Ended up going),**일하게 됐어요** (Came to work),**먹게 됐어요** (Came to eat),**만나게 됐어요** (Ended up meeting)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **가게 됐어요.** (I ended up going.)
- **일하게 됐어요.** (I came to work.)
- **먹게 됐어요.** (I came to eat.)
- **만나게 됐어요.** (I ended up meeting.)
- **살게 됐어요.** (I ended up living there.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **다음 달에 한국으로 출장을 가게 됐어요.** (I ended up going on a business trip to Korea next month.)
- **그 사람과 우연히 같은 회사에서 일하게 되었어요.** (I happened to work at the same company as that person by chance.)
- **매운 음식을 잘 먹게 됐어요.** (I became able to eat spicy food well.)
- **친구 소개로 남자친구를 만나게 됐어요.** (I ended up meeting my boyfriend through a friend’s introduction.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip**
> 
> 
> It’s also used when humbly bragging about your improved skills. Like “요즘 한국어를 더 잘하게 됐어요” (I’ve become better at Korean lately)!
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Describe a change in situation.

1. (I liked Korean idols, and before I knew it, I ended up studying Korean) 한국 아이돌을 좋아하다 보니까 어느새 한국어를 _______________. (공부하다)
2. (Something came up, so I ended up not being able to go to the party tomorrow) 사정이 생겨서 내일 파티에 못 _______________. (가다)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 공부하게 됐어요 / 2. 가게 됐어요

### 9) My Sentence

Is there something you started doing or a connection you made by chance in your life?

- 저는 우연히 _______________게 됐어요.

## 

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_58.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 형용사 어간 Adjective Stem ]** +**아/어지다**
- *“Become [Adjective] / Get [Adjective]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Meeting an adjective, it indicates that a certain state is changing ‘gradually’ as time passes. It’s very useful for describing any process of a state changing, such as the weather getting colder, your mood improving, or getting prettier.

### 3) Grammar

Use it ONLY with **Adjectives**! If the vowel is ‘ㅏ, ㅗ’, use**‘-아지다’**. Otherwise, use**‘-어지다’**. ‘하다’ changes to**‘해지다’**. Usually expressing a result or a progression, use the past tense**‘-아/어졌어요’** or the progressive form**‘-아/어지고 있어요’**.

### 4) How it’s used

- Changes in weather: “날씨가 많이 따뜻해졌어요.”
- Improvement in skills: “한국어 실력이 좋아졌어요.”
- Complimenting appearance: “얼굴이 예뻐졌어요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**따뜻해졌어요** (Became warm),**좋아졌어요** (Became good/improved),**넓어져요** (Getting wider),**예뻐졌어요** (Became pretty)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **따뜻해졌어요.** (Became warm.)
- **좋아졌어요.** (Improved / Became good.)
- **넓어져요.** (Getting wider.)
- **예뻐졌어요.** (Became pretty.)
- **추워졌어요.** (Became cold.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **날씨가 많이 따뜻해졌어요.** (The weather has become much warmer.)
- **한국어 실력이 좋아졌어요.** (My Korean skills have improved.)
- **길이 점점 넓어져요.** (The road is gradually getting wider.)
- **화장을 지우니까 얼굴이 예뻐졌어요.** (After taking off makeup, my face became prettier.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip**
> 
> 
> It’s a perfect match with the adverb **‘점점 (jeom-jeom, gradually)’**. If you say “날씨가 점점 추워져요”, the passage of time is felt perfectly.
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Describe the change of state.

1. (I cleaned my room, and it became very clean) 방 청소를 하고 났더니 방이 아주 _______________. (깨끗하다)
2. (Autumn has come, and the weather has become much cooler) 가을이 되어서 날씨가 많이 _______________. (시원하다)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 깨끗해졌어요 / 2. 시원해졌어요

### 9) My Sentence

What changes have been happening to yourself or around you lately?

- 요즘 _______________이/가 점점 _______________아/어지고 있어요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_59.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 형용사/동사 어간 Adjective/Verb Stem ]** +**을/ㄹ 뻔했어요**
- *“Almost [Verb]ed / Nearly [Verb]ed”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Used when something almost happened in the past, but fortunately (or unfortunately) it didn’t actually happen in reality. It’s great for describing those dizzying moments when your heart dropped because you almost fell or were almost late.

### 3) Grammar

If no Batchim or ‘ㄹ’ Batchim, use **‘-ㄹ 뻔했어요’**. If Batchim, use**‘-을 뻔했어요’**. It goes much better with verbs than adjectives. Since you are recalling a dangerous moment that has already passed, it is ALWAYS used only in the**past tense**.

### 4) How it’s used

- When avoiding a crisis: “넘어질 뻔했어요.”
- When escaping a mistake: “비행기를 놓칠 뻔했어요.”
- When exaggerating: “배고파 죽을 뻔했어요.” (Because you didn’t actually die!)

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**넘어질 뻔했어요** (Almost fell),**놓칠 뻔했어요** (Almost missed),**큰일 날 뻔했어요** (Almost had a big trouble),**죽을 뻔했어요** (Almost died)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **넘어질 뻔했어요.** (Almost fell.)
- **놓칠 뻔했어요.** (Almost missed.)
- **큰일 날 뻔했어요.** (Almost had a big trouble.)
- **울 뻔했어요.** (Almost cried.)
- **죽을 뻔했어요.** (Almost died - Exaggeration)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **길이 미끄러워서 넘어질 뻔했어요.** (The road was slippery so I almost fell down.)
- **늦잠을 자서 비행기를 놓칠 뻔했어요.** (I overslept and almost missed the flight.)
- **휴대폰을 안 가져와서 큰일 날 뻔했어요.** (I didn’t bring my phone, so we were almost in big trouble.)
- **너무 웃겨서 울 뻔했어요.** (It was so funny I almost cried.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip**
> 
> 
> If you use the adverb **“하마터면 (ha-ma-teo-myeon)”** at the very front of the sentence, the dizzying feeling is maximized. If you say “하마터면 늦을 뻔했어요!”, it’s perfect native Korean.
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Describe a dizzying moment.

1. (I was running across the crosswalk and almost dropped my phone) 횡단보도를 뛰어가다가 휴대폰을 _______________. (떨어뜨리다)
2. (I watched a horror movie yesterday, and it was so scary my heart almost stopped) 어제 공포 영화를 봤는데 너무 무서워서 심장이 _______________. (멈추다)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 떨어뜨릴 뻔했어요 / 2. 멈출 뻔했어요

### 9) My Sentence

Recall an experience you recently had where you ‘almost got into big trouble’ and write it down.

- 어제 하마터면 _______________을/ㄹ 뻔했어요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_60.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사 어간 Verb Stem ]** +**을/ㄹ 걸 그랬어요**
- *“I should have [Verb]ed (but I didn’t)”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Used when you deeply regret an action you did NOT do in the past, beating the ground and saying “Ah… I should have done that!”. (If you are regretting an action you ‘did’ do, you use “-지 말 걸 그랬어요 (I shouldn’t have…)”.)

### 3) Grammar

If no Batchim or ‘ㄹ’ Batchim, attach **‘-ㄹ 걸 그랬어요’**. If Batchim, attach**‘-을 걸 그랬어요’**.

### 4) How it’s used

- When realizing a better choice too late: “우산을 가져올 걸 그랬어요.”
- When regretting not preparing in advance: “공부를 더 할 걸 그랬어요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**가져올 걸 그랬어요** (Should have brought),**할 걸 그랬어요** (Should have done),**말 걸 그랬어요** (Shouldn’t have done),**살 걸 그랬어요** (Should have bought)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **가져올 걸 그랬어요.** (Should have brought.)
- **할 걸 그랬어요.** (Should have done.)
- **말 걸 그랬어요.** (Shouldn’t have done.)
- **살 걸 그랬어요.** (Should have bought.)
- **갈 걸 그랬어요.** (Should have gone.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **비가 오네요. 우산을 가져올 걸 그랬어요.** (It’s raining. I should have brought an umbrella.)
- **시험이 너무 어려웠어요. 공부를 더 할 걸 그랬어요.** (The test was too difficult. I should have studied more.)
- **밥을 먹지 말 걸 그랬어요. 배가 너무 아파요.** (I shouldn’t have eaten. My stomach hurts so much.)
- **그 신발을 살 걸 그랬어요. 계속 생각나요.** (I should have bought those shoes. I keep thinking about them.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip**
> 
> 
> Among close friends, people very often drop “그랬어요” and just say **“-을/ㄹ 걸!”** while scratching the back of their head like talking to themselves. Try practicing it with emotion, saying “아, 그냥 갈걸!” (Ah, I should have just gone!).
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Regret a past choice.

1. (Upon hearing the news that the party was so much fun) 아, 나도 어제 파티에 _______________. (가다)
2. (When your face is puffy after eating Ramen at night) 어젯밤에 라면을 먹지 _______________. (말다)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 갈 걸 그랬어요 / 2. 말 걸 그랬어요

### 9) My Sentence

Is there anything you regretted recently? If you could turn back time, what would you have done?

- 아, 그때 _______________을/ㄹ 걸 그랬어요.


---


![[survival_mission_5.png|700]]


![[chapter_06.png|700]]

![[sulsul_pattern_61.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 명사 Noun ]** +**때문에** /**[ 동사/형용사 Verb/Adjective Stem ]** +**기 때문에**
- *“Because of [Noun]” / “Because [Verb/Adjective]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Used when you want to explain the clear reason or cause for a certain result very distinctly. It feels much more logical and definitive than ‘아/어서’ or ‘니까’. It is mainly used when giving an objective reason or making an excuse that is not your fault.

### 3) Grammar

- Attach **‘때문에’** right after a**Noun**. (e.g., 비 때문에)
- Attach**‘-기 때문에’** after a**Verb or Adjective Stem**. (e.g., 바쁘기 때문에)
- You can also use it perfectly after a past tense marker! (e.g., 늦게 잤기 때문에)

### 4) How it’s used

- When blaming a cause: “비 때문에 차가 막혔어요.”
- When stating a definitive reason: “일이 많기 때문에 바빠요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**비 때문에** (Because of rain),**눈 때문에** (Because of snow),**일 때문에** (Because of work),**아프기 때문에** (Because I’m sick)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **비 때문에** (Because of rain)
- **일 때문에** (Because of work)
- **너 때문에** (Because of you)
- **바쁘기 때문에** (Because I’m busy)
- **늦었기 때문에** (Because I was late)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **비 때문에 늦었어요.** (I was late because of the rain.)
- **일이 많기 때문에 주말에도 출근해요.** (I go to work even on weekends because I have a lot of work.)
- **어제 늦게 잤기 때문에 너무 피곤해요.** (I am tired because I slept late yesterday.)
- **눈 때문에 비행기가 취소됐어요.** (The flight was canceled because of the snow.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip**
> 
> 
> Native speakers use ‘Noun + 때문에’ all the time when blaming a negative result. Try playfully saying “다 너 때문에!” (It’s all because of you!) with full intonation!
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Choose the correct form to explain the reason.

1. (It snowed a lot. That’s why the road is congested) 눈 _______________ 길이 막혀요.
2. (I don’t have time. That’s why I took a taxi) 시간이 없_______________ 택시를 탔어요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 때문에 / 2. 기 때문에

### 9) My Sentence

Did your plans change recently because of a certain event or person?

- 저는 어제 _______________ 때문에 _______________.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_62.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동작 동사 Action Verb Stem ]** +**느라고**
- *“Because I was doing [Verb] (I couldn’t do something else)”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is a magical excuse pattern used when you spent all your time doing the first action, so you couldn’t do the second action that you were supposed to do. It sounds very natural when you want to appeal to your unfairness, like “I didn’t skip it on purpose, I was busy doing THAT!”.

### 3) Grammar

- It ONLY attaches to **Action Verbs**! It cannot be used with adjectives. (e.g., 바쁘느라고 - X)
- The subject (Me) of the first sentence and the second sentence must be exactly the same.
- A negative result usually follows, such as ‘~못 했어요’ (couldn’t do) or ‘~늦었어요’ (was late).

### 4) How it’s used

- When you couldn’t answer the phone: “회의하느라고 못 받았어요.”
- When you were late for an appointment: “일하느라고 늦었어요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**일하느라고** (Because I was working),**공부하느라고** (Because I was studying),**보느라고** (Because I was watching)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **일하느라고** (Because I was working…)
- **공부하느라고** (Because I was studying…)
- **준비하느라고** (Because I was preparing…)
- **찾느라고** (Because I was looking for it…)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **공부하느라고 전화를 못 받았어요.** (I couldn’t answer the phone because I was studying.)
- **일하느라고 밥을 못 먹었어요.** (I couldn’t eat because I was working.)
- **드라마를 보느라고 늦게 잤어요.** (I slept late because I was watching a drama.)
- **길을 찾느라고 고생했어요.** (I had a hard time because I was looking for the way.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip**
> 
> 
> In conversation, people often drop the ‘-고’ and just say **‘공부하느라’**, **‘일하느라’**. If you use it with an unfair facial expression when making an excuse, your acting gets an A+!
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Make a real excuse.

1. (Situation where you couldn’t do homework because you were playing games) 게임을 _______________ 숙제를 못 했어요.
2. (Situation where you couldn’t eat lunch because you were having a meeting) 회의_______________ 밥을 못 먹었어요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 하느라고 (or 하느라) / 2. 하느라고 (or 하느라)

### 9) My Sentence

Have you ever missed something you had to do because you were deeply absorbed in something else?

- 저는 _______________느라고 _______________못 했어요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_63.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사/형용사 Verb/Adjective Stem ]** +**거나**
- **[ 명사 Noun ]** +** (이)나**
- *“Or” / “Either A or B”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Used when selecting one out of two or more options. It is an expression that perfectly replaces the English ‘Or’ when you coolly suggest or list things like “This or that?”.

### 3) Grammar

- **Verbs, Adjectives:** Unconditionally attach**‘-거나’** to the stem regardless of the Batchim.
- **Nouns:** If there is a Batchim, attach**‘-이나’**; if there is no Batchim, attach**‘-나’**. (e.g., 주말이나, 휴가나)

### 4) How it’s used

- When listing two actions: “주말에 영화를 보거나 책을 읽어요.”
- When giving choices: “버스나 지하철을 타세요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**보거나** (Watch or),**먹거나** (Eat or),**바쁘거나** (Busy or),**버스나** (Bus or),**커피나** (Coffee or)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **가거나** (Go or)
- **먹거나** (Eat or)
- **크거나** (Big or)
- **산이나 바다** (Mountain or sea)
- **커피나 차** (Coffee or tea)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **주말에 영화를 보거나 책을 읽어요.** (I watch a movie or read a book on weekends.)
- **버스나 지하철을 타세요.** (Take a bus or the subway.)
- **피곤하거나 아프면 집에서 쉬세요.** (Rest if you are tired or sick.)
- **내일 아침이나 점심에 만나요.** (Let’s meet tomorrow morning or at noon.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Connect the two options.

1. (I will take the subway or a taxi) 지하철_______________ 택시를 타고 갈 거예요.
2. (I listen to music or watch TV) 음악을 _______________ 텔레비전을 봐요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 이나 / 2. 듣거나

### 9) My Sentence

What are the two actions you usually choose to do when you’re stressed?

- 저는 _______________거나 _______________해요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_64.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 동사/형용사 Verb/Adjective Stem ]** +**든지**
- *“Whether (you do this) or (do that)”*

### 2) Core Meaning

It’s great to use when you want to give off the vibe, “Do whatever you want, I don’t care!”. It’s a pattern showing tolerance(?), meaning that no matter which option you choose among many, the result is the same, or it’s fine either way.

### 3) Grammar

- Attach **‘-든지’** to the Verb or Adjective Stem regardless of the Batchim.
- Usually, opposite words are listed and used like pairs in the form of**‘-든지 -든지’**. (e.g., 오든지 말든지 / Whether you come or not)
- When combined with question words (누구, 언제, 어디), it gives the feeling of ‘Any’. (e.g., 언제든지 / Anytime)

### 4) How it’s used

- When saying you don’t care: “먹든지 말든지 마음대로 해.”
- When welcoming anytime: “언제든지 전화하세요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**언제든지** (Anytime),**어디든지** (Anywhere),**누구든지** (Anyone),**하든지 말든지** (Whether do it or not)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **가든지 말든지** (Whether go or not)
- **먹든지 말든지** (Whether eat or not)
- **비싸든지 싸든지** (Whether expensive or cheap)
- **언제든지** (Anytime)
- **어디든지** (Anywhere)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **먹든지 말든지 마음대로 하세요.** (Do whatever you want, whether you eat or not.)
- **도움이 필요하면 언제든지 전화하세요.** (Call me anytime if you need help.)
- **비싸든지 싸든지 꼭 살 거예요.** (I will definitely buy it whether it’s expensive or cheap.)
- **누구든지 참가할 수 있어요.** (Anyone can participate.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip**
> 
> 
> Native speakers sometimes change ‘-든지’ to **‘-든가’** to speak more comfortably. Saying “먹든가 말든가!” (Eat or don’t eat!) gives a slightly blunter and more casual feel.
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Give them a free choice.

1. (Watch the movie, or don’t watch it, you decide) 영화를 _______________ 말_______________ 결정하세요.
2. (Any time is fine) _______________ 오세요. (언제)
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 보든지, 든지 / 2. 언제든지

### 9) My Sentence

Open your heart and tell the other person that they can contact you anytime.

- 괜찮으니까 _______________ 연락하세요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_65.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **아무** +**[ 명사 Noun ]** +**도** /**아무것도** +**[ 부정어 Negative ]**
- *“Not any [Noun]” / “Nothing” / “No one”*

### 2) Core Meaning

This is an expression used to strongly negate everything, saying “There isn’t a single one, it’s zero!”. This pattern is the best when you want to firmly state that there is absolutely no one, or nothing, without any exceptions.

### 3) Grammar

- For people, use **‘아무도’** (없어요/안 해요), and for things, use**‘아무것도’** (없어요/안 해요).
- For places, it is written as**‘아무 데도’** (안 가요).
- The most important thing: A negative expression (없어요, 안 해요, 못 해요) MUST follow this pattern to become a complete negation!

### 4) How it’s used

- When a space is empty: “아무도 없어요.”
- When you haven’t done anything: “아무것도 안 먹었어요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**아무도** (No one),**아무것도** (Nothing),**아무 데도** (Nowhere),**아무 말도** (Not a single word)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **아무도 없어요.** (There is no one.)
- **아무것도 없어요.** (There is nothing.)
- **아무 데도 안 가요.** (I’m going nowhere.)
- **아무 말도 안 했어요.** (I didn’t say anything.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **불이 꺼져 있고 교실에 아무도 없어요.** (The lights are off and there is no one in the classroom.)
- **다이어트 중이라서 오늘 아무것도 안 먹었어요.** (I’m on a diet so I ate nothing today.)
- **주말에 너무 피곤해서 아무 데도 안 갈 거예요.** (I’m so tired on the weekend I won’t go anywhere.)
- **비밀이니까 아무한테도 말하지 마세요.** (It’s a secret, so don’t tell anyone.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip**
> 
> 
> When you’re really hungry, you say “아무거나 주세요” (Give me anything), right? If you attach **‘나’** instead of ‘도’, the meaning flips 180 degrees to mean “Anything is okay (Anything)!”
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Create a perfect ‘0’ with total negation.

1. (There is not even 1 person in the room) 방 안에 _______________ 없어요.
2. (I haven’t eaten a single thing since morning) 아침부터 _______________ 안 먹었어요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 아무도 / 2. 아무것도

### 9) My Sentence

State your plans while lying in bed on a really tiring weekend.

- 저는 오늘 _______________ 안 할 거예요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_66.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 명사 Noun ]** +**밖에 없어요 / 밖에 안 (못)**
- *“There is only [Noun]” / “I only did [Noun]” (emphasizing lack)*

### 2) Core Meaning

Rather than simply saying “I only have this (Only)”, this is used when you speak full of emotion saying, “It’s a shame/it’s not enough because I ONLY have this barely”. It is a favorite pattern of Koreans that strongly emits a sense of lack or regret!

### 3) Grammar

- It attaches after a noun, and a negative expression (‘없어요’, ‘안’, ‘못’, etc.) MUST always follow it.
- It can NEVER be used with positive verbs (있어요, 해요)! (e.g., 하나밖에 있어요 - X)

### 4) How it’s used

- When short on money or time: “시간이 5분밖에 안 남았어요.”
- When regretting a small quantity: “사과가 하나밖에 없어요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**조금밖에** (Only a little),**하나밖에** (Only one),**천 원밖에** (Only 1,000 won)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **하나밖에 없어요.** (There is only one.)
- **천 원밖에 없어요.** (I only have 1,000 won.)
- **조금밖에 못 잤어요.** (I could only sleep a little.)
- **한 명밖에 안 왔어요.** (Only one person came.)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **지갑에 돈이 천 원밖에 없어요.** (I have nothing but 1,000 won in my wallet.)
- **시험이 내일인데 시간이 5분밖에 안 남았어요.** (The exam is tomorrow and there are only 5 minutes left.)
- **저기요, 사과를 세 개 시켰는데 한 개밖에 안 주셨어요.** (Excuse me, I ordered three apples but you only gave me one.)
- **한국어를 조금밖에 못 해요.** (I can only speak a little Korean.)

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Express a lack or regret.

1. (I slept 3 hours a day. It’s too lacking) 어젯밤에 3시간_______________ 못 잤어요.
2. (There is just one lone bottle of water in the fridge) 냉장고에 물 _______________ 없어요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 밖에 / 2. 밖에

### 9) My Sentence

What is something you have right now that is much less than you expected, making you feel regretful?

- 저는 지금 _______________밖에 없어요.

---

 

![[sulsul_pattern_67.png|500]]
### 1) Pattern Definition

- **[ 명사 Noun ]** +**에 비해서 / 에 비하면**
- *“Compared to [Noun]”*

### 2) Core Meaning

Used when comparing two things. It’s similar to ‘보다 더’ which we learned earlier, but it feels much more natural when something far exceeds your expectations, or when comparing against a clear ‘standard’ you’ve set. It’s a great pattern for evaluating cost-effectiveness, like “이 가격에 비해서 품질이 완전 좋아!” (Compared to this price, the quality is completely awesome!).

### 3) Grammar

- Attach it right after a noun.
- You can also change a verb to a noun form and use the **‘-(으)ㄴ/는 것에 비해서’** structure.
- ‘비해서’ and ‘비하면’ have almost identical meanings, so you can swap them depending on your mood.

### 4) How it’s used

- When evaluating cost-effectiveness: “가격에 비해서 양이 많아요.”
- When comparing with last year/yesterday: “작년에 비해서 많이 추워요.”

### 5) High-Frequency Words

**가격에 비해서** (Compared to the price),**나이에 비해서** (Compared to the age),**생각한 것에 비해서** (Compared to what I thought)

### 6) Basic Examples (Survival Level)

- **가격에 비해서** (Compared to the price)
- **나이에 비해서** (Compared to the age)
- **어제에 비해서** (Compared to yesterday)
- **작년에 비해서** (Compared to last year)

### 7) Expansion Examples (Natural Level)

- **이 식당은 가격에 비해서 양이 정말 많아요.** (Compared to the price, the portion is really large at this restaurant.)
- **작년에 비해서 올해 날씨가 훨씬 따뜻해요.** (Compared to last year, the weather is much warmer this year.)
- **그 배우는 나이에 비해서 어려 보여요.** (That actor looks young compared to his age.)
- **공부한 것에 비해서 시험을 잘 봤어요.** (I did well on the test compared to what I studied.)
> ![[pink_mascot.png|60]] **Teacher’s Tip**
> 
> 
> When speaking a bit fast, some people drop the particle and say “어제 비해서~” instead of “어제에 비해서~”, but you must keep the **‘에’** alive to speak very accurate and high-class Korean!
> 

### 8) Practice

✔**Practice 1.** Compare against a standard.

1. (Korean is easier than the standard I expected) 제가 생각한 것_______________ 한국어가 쉬워요.
2. (The electricity bill is higher this month compared to last month) 지난달_______________ 전기요금이 많이 나왔어요.
> ✏️ **Answer Key**
> 
> 1. 에 비해서 (or 에 비하면) / 2. 에 비해서 (or 에 비하면)

### 9) My Sentence

Do you have any special traits compared to your age? (Looking younger, acting mature, etc.!)

- 저는 제 나이_______________ _______________.

---

## 💎 Special Unit 6. 나이와 존댓말의 세계 (The World of Age and Honorifics)
